And now there was Roger; Roger, who was different from other men; Roger, who even in his love for her was different. Even whilst doubting herself, the still, small voice that was her second ego had cried insistently: "Take him marry him ... he loves you. there may • · never be another chance."

Since their engagement five months ago Anne had analyzed Roger and found that he possessed that greatest of qualities in the potential husband-steadfast reliability. 

Apart from that he was a rather ordinary, cold sort of person, who regarded any display of sentiment with the [ 14 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

typical Englishman's embarrassment, so that with him every sense was subdued every enthusiasm controlled. Analyzing her own feelings, Anne knew that, despite her respect and admiration for Roger, she was not really in love with him; that she felt for him only a calm, steady affection which could never be defined as love. But she supposed, philosophically, that it would be rather dreadful to be very deeply in love with one's husband because one would be so often hurt. 

To Anne, marriage spelt finality. The prospect of it was wonderful, she agreed, but the realization of it a series of disappointments-lessened, it is true, if one had money, but further aggravated if one were poor. ·· • And then one foggy morning Anne had flown from London and Roger. The latter was rather annoyed about the invitation from Aunt Lillian; had said, with some impatience: "Why are you so restless, Anne? Why on earth must you fly off like this a few weeks before our marriage?" .. 

Anne hadn't known why, except a vague desire to fly from Autumn fogs and various other irritating influences. 

At the back of her mind she hoped that somewhere, somehow, she might capture the threads of Adventure bright, colorful, elusive threads that in seizing she would become transported to another world. Something glorious to look back upon ... and remember. Through the vista of years there had been no great moments in Anne's life; all had flown on a smooth plane wherein time had passed punctuated only by a series of dinners and dances, tennis parties, the buying of expensive clothes, the vague and promiscuous love affairs.

Anne sighed. The memories, crowding in upon her, [ 15 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

were blurred by a sudden sharp thought; it was a week since she had written to Roger. A small pile of his letters stared at her in mute reproach from the writing desk. 

She rose from the bed, drawing her filmy georgette wrap closely about her as she crossed to the desk and sat down. 

She wrote to Roger, never pausing to think very deeply; glancing up now and again to catch her green and gold reflection in the wide mirror opposite. 

"DARLING: 

"I haven't written to you for a week, I know; but

I am rather bored with this place after all, and cannot find anything very interesting to write about. 

You'd hate it here . . . it's too bright . . . too unreal. "To-morrow the carnival starts, and that will be pretty ghastly-all garlic, foolish processions and confetti; something like the Charity balls to which I frequently drag you, only in this case the whole town joins in. A tall, thin woman with sleek dark hair and a youthful figure came slowly across the room. "Hullo, Lillian." "I wondered what had happened to you, Anne. At the head of the table, an imposing figure in gold brocade, sat Lillian, with Adolphe, short, slim and swarthy, on her left. At the further end sat a silent and subdued Anne, dressed in a thin black frock, listening politely to the spasmodic conversation of the weedy youth on her right hand, and hating it all. Sitting there, it struck her forcibly that these friends of Lillian's were all of a type—the men, tall and small-waisted, epigrammatical; the women, slim and ageless, with short hair, the interminable cigarette, lacquered mouths. They ranged down each side of the table, all wearing just the right shade of boredom, each conversing with a neighbor in a voice loud enough to be not quite good form, each passing the usual conventional remarks, and never by any chance being so rude as to be natural.

These so-called modernists, thought Anne, were amazingly dull... one imagined them in a scene similar to this as the social background of a Maugham play, discussing just the same stereotyped subjects punctuated by witty epigrams sex-plays... modern morals. free love . . . all combined in the modern school of thought. · · "You are very quiet to-night, Miss Trevor."

Roubaix with scarcely veiled contempt. "I like the Italians, they are passionate in all things, and very sincere; the French disgust me because they are so obviously the reverse." 

Anne played bridge badly that night, restless and troubled by vague thoughts of Roger. As soon as she was able to slip away, she hurried out into the cool night air.

A white crescent moon drifted in an arc of midnight blue. Beneath it the lights of Monaco Harbor stabbed the darkness like soft yellow eyes. Along the dusty white roads, running like strips of ribbon down to the beach and promenades, gleaming luxury cars loomed up like giant monsters and split the stillness of night with the throb of engines and the long, warning cry of their horns. The exotic perfume of passion flowers came to Anne as she walked in the quiet garden against the low wall overlooking the bay great masses of pink azaleas touched her cheek with dewy petals as she lingered. 

A sea-gull, molten silver from beak to tail in the moonlight, screeched along the water line and flung the foamcrested waves into diamond spray. 

Over Anne's shoulder a still, small voice cried and cried: "To-morrow there is always to-morrow.' 

The wind from the sea blew across her face... it smelt of salt, and space, and adventure. [ 20 ]

HE Riviera Club seethed with activity. The tennis courts were besieged by eager white-clad players; the pavilion and verandah buzzed with soundlaughter-the shuffling of busy waiters-the rustle of letters and morning papers-the blur of conversation, sotto voce, among the knots of people discussing the scandal of the hour. 

The Danvers twins, playing with Norah and Hugh

Her pen flew over the white pages, and this time Anne did not once glance up to catch her green and gold reflection in the mirror opposite. [ 26 ]

-2- At three o'clock that same afternoon the house party from the Villa Florale were gathered on the balcony of the Hôtel de Paris, watching the opening of the annual carnival. The promenade below was lined with people, all armed with bags of confetti and strings of flowers. 

Down the bay, slim white yachts, gaily decorated, lay anchored close to shore. The whole town was a riot of color; the streets festooned with flowers and hung with fairy lights; the promenade ablaze with decorated chariots and camouflaged cars and the grotesque figures of the procession.

Leaning on the balcony, Anne was seized with a sudden desire to go down and join in the gaiety below; to drown herself down there in the human tide, with the mad music and the shouts of the people dinning in her ears.

Without attracting the attention of either Lillian or her friends, she slipped past them and ran down into the lounge below. Swiftly and deliberately she made her way through the crowded foyer and so out into the teeming mass on the other side of the plate-glass doors. 

That she was mad to attempt such a thing, Anne knew; but the sudden thrill that ran through her body spurred her on, and then, with the seething crowd around her, it was too late to return. She was aware of the tumult of voices; the shuffling of many feet; the ribald laughter of a carnival-mad crowd. And then she heard a voice above the rest, a wild, desperate cry that rose on a single note of fear. "Anne! For God's sake..." [ 27 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

She looked up, saw Hugh Randall's agonized face from the balcony above, and smiled. She lost sight of him as the crowd lifted her, hatless and breathless, and swirled her far from the Hôtel de Paris.

The whole town roared about her like a flood. The figures of the procession, wierd and grotesque, towered above her head. She saw the scattered flowers, the snowstorm of confetti, the bright dresses of the girls and children; heard the wild crash of laughter and cheering; felt the sickening proximity of warm, damp bodies. 

And then Anne was suddenly afraid! The promise of adventure died, leaving in its place only a stark fear. She struggled to free herself from the surging crowd about her, conscious of an overwhelming weariness that threatened to envelop her. Her body sagged, her head ached, her voice almost failed her as she cried for help; but the mad crowd pushed on, ruthless and uncaring. She closed her eyes; felt the salt tears on her lips; prayed as she had never done before that something might happen to release her from the agony that overpowered her. 

The blazing sunlight was obscured momentarily by the passing of a giant gondola, hideously painted, and drawn by a dozen red-sashed Italians. 

Anne, half-opening her eyes because the sudden cool shadow brought relief, saw the wildly leaping figures in the boat singing and dancing and throwing absurdly crude flowers.

The crowd pressed closer and closer about her; Anne felt her feet bruised under heavy boots; her flimsy frock torn from her shoulders. She was aware of gradual suffocation, and summoning every ounce of strength, she [ 28 ]

WHITE PIERROT cried aloud at the top of her voice; a queer, half strangled lost in the tumult of voices about her. cry

Looking back afterwards she vaguely remembered the flood of sunlight that burst over her again as the gondola slowly passed; she remembered, more vaguely still, the glimpse of a solitary figure poised dangerously on the highest point at the stern of the boat: a white pierrot, with a painted skull face!

The rest was like a dream, something only half remembered yet never quite forgotten. A flying figure, vividly white in the dusty sunshine; a string of muttered oaths as that figure landed, breathless, beside Anne and flung a careless arm about her; a voice, gay but kind, that whispered in her ear: "Just hold on to me. I'll get you out of this." 

Anne awoke to full consciousness, aware that her head rested against a broad expanse of white satin, whilst her feet barely trailed the ground. The crowd had thinned about them. Under foot there lay mimosa and wet violets and bright carnations trodden full of dust.

Above her Anne saw the painted skull face over a stiff white ruffle-a sad face, but behind it dark eyes laughed at her.

Away from the crowded promenade the white pierrot led her to a quiet street, long since deserted by the carnival makers, where the only signs of life were a few sleepy-eyed cats in the sunny doorways. Half way up the street a gleaming Hispano Suiza stood against the curb, its shining blue and silver body the only thing of beauty in a drab and deserted byway.

CHAPTER IV 

ITHOUT really understanding the subtle difference between the man who was well bred and the bounder who pretended to be, Anne knew it when she met it.

She came through the swinging doors of the Hôtel de Paris at nine o'clock that evening and found Pierrot waiting in the foyer. She saw him, a sleek young man in evening clothes as opposed to that breathless being caught with her in the whirl of carnival; realized, instinctively, that he was a gentleman. 

His eyes flattered as he appraised her without seeming to stare unduly. She wore a filmy white frock with a small tight-fitting bodice, the skirt being a cloud of tulle and wispy georgette. A bunch of pale violets lay against her shoulder. Her hair, short and very golden, fell across her forehead in soft waves. She looked rather tired, shadowy-eyed and elusive.

They were shown their table, a small one tucked away in a corner by the windows overlooking the bay and the gaily lighted promenade below. The restaurant was crowded with fashionable diners, and sounds of laughter and the popping of champagne corks came from all sides and threatened to drown the lively music played by a negro band.

Anne felt absurdly happy. She laughed as she picked up a small bunch of white flowers that lay against her [ 32 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

plate, and pinned them with the violets on her shoulder. "You must tell me your name," she said, still smiling. "I can't possibly have dinner with someone whose name 

I don't even know," she added.

Pierrot bowed across the table, amused and deferential. "Oh, dear, that is your terrible English convention again. I rather wondered if it would prevent your dining with me." "It nearly did." "And what made you dare to accept my invitation?" "I don't know... I think I felt a little reckless." 

They both laughed at that, and Anne said, irrelevantly: "You don't believe in convention, then? You think we should all fly about doing exactly what we please, regardless of the consequences?" "Convention is the highest point of civilization. It is ingrained in us all until it becomes a lever for restricting one's natural and impulsive actions." "A good thing, too," laughed Anne. It came as something of a shock to her to realize that Pierrot was not an Englishman. She wondered if he were French, Italian, or Spanish . . . the question absorbed her as she finished the last spoonful of iced melon. "You still haven't told me your name," she said at last. "My name is Rollo Savini." 

Anne thought, with inward amusement: "Sounds like musical comedy." Aloud, she murmured: "My name is 

Anne Trevor." She wondered whether he would remark, foolishly, as most young men did: "Pretty name, Anne,” or, worse still: “Anne suits you perfectly." But Rollo said nothing except: "Now that is all settled." "Are you Italian?" asked Anne, later. [ 33 ]

WHITE PIERROT "Half, my mother was an Englishwoman. And now, tell me about yourself." "No," said Anne, quickly, "I hate talking about myself.

Now that we know each other's names we'll pretend we've known each other ages and ages. But tell me first how you came to see me in all that crowd this afternoon ?" "I don't really know . . . it was extraordinary. I could see that you were very desperate and I imagined you being crushed to death in another moment. It was better luck than judgment that landed me right at your your side...." "It was wonderful," breathed Anne. "I think I should have fainted in another moment and goodness knows what would have happened to me then. Of course, I was mad to leave my people, but I wanted to see what the carnival was like at first hand." "The thirst for adventure, I suppose?" smiled Rollo.

The words, lightly spoken, brought Anne back to her talk with Hugh Randall the previous day; brought her back, too, to the memory of her walk in the quiet garden of the villa a few hours later, when she had leaned against the seaward wall looking out across the water with the salt breeze ruffling her hair and the still, small voice within her saying: "To-morrow... there is always to-morrow." 

To-morrow had dawned and brought Anne an answer to her unspoken prayer in the shape of a white pierrot with a sad skull-face and laughing eyes who had now materialized into the very presentable young man opposite her. "And I found adventure," said Anne at last. "I'm glad it. it happened." · •· • "Thank you, Anne,” replied Rollo gravely. There was [ 34 ]

WHITE 

PIERROT 

no cool presumption in his use of her Christian name; his attitude was merely one of boyish friendship, made possible by the incident of the afternoon.

They talked of plays, books, and people and found that they failed to agree upon almost every subject. Anne was highly amused, realizing that in Rollo there was a depth of character that would not allow him to surrender his own conviction upon any subject for the sake of simplifying an argument.

Dinner over, Rollo hung her cloak about her shoulders, and they left the hotel. The Hispano Suiza, with its expressionless chauffeur, awaited them at the entrance, and, jumping into it, they slid away in the direction of the Casino.

Through the magnificent gardens they went, and on leaving the car, mounted the countless steps of the Casino and disappeared through the heavy doors so closely guarded by massive commissionaires. 

The ballroom was ablaze with the brilliance of vivid fancy dress, colored lights, and banks of wonderful flowers. From the band there came the long, looping wail of the tango.

Rollo found room for them in a corner of one of the boxes, and then fetched Anne some champagne. Anne knew that two glasses at dinner followed by Cointreau had been her limit, but she felt genuinely thirsty and the bubbling wine tempted her.

The lights were lowered and colored beams played on the dancers and on the revolving ball covered with tiny squares of mirror that hung from the middle of the ceiling.

The wine had warmed her, confused her a little, and [ 35 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

robbed her of that first self-consciousness. All about them there was music and laughter and the sobbing tune of the tango. "Shall we dance?"

She heard Pierrot's voice above her and, looking up, she smiled. They moved on to the floor and were swallowed up by sounds and darkness, only a gleam of purple and red picking them out as they drifted amongst the dancers. 

There came showers of confetti, a snowstorm of color, and under it lips met lips and nobody cared. In the sob and shuffle of the tune about them hung a spell, for the tune was a melody, a fragment of song, whistled by a white pierrot with a sad skull face and laughing eyes.

The song ended on a low note that died away in the darkness.

Rollo, with his cheek bent against Anne's, felt her quiver in his arms. "I love you, Anne.” "Rollo," whispered Anne, and there was a queer little catch in her voice. And all the time a voice kept saying, "This isn't real things like this only happen in plays and novels hours.. 99 · • you've known him only a few

But the lights went up and he was still at her side, smiling and holding one of her hands curled up in his own.

WHITE PIERROT

They went out into the cool night air where the moon hung low in a velvet sky and shimmered in an unbroken pathway across the calm, dark sea. All about there lay the last remnants of the carnival; scattered flowers, broken and dust trodden; confetti, bright fragments shaken from the shoulders of passersby; hundreds of looped fairy lights that illumined the darkness with their soft pastel brightness.

The Hispano Suiza crept up to them, luxurious with the deep softness of its cushions and the pale glow of rose-colored light within. "You have a beautiful car," said Anne sleepily, appreciating its comfort for the second time that day.

Rollo seemed scarcely to hear; he lay back under the rug and passing lights showed his face alive and keen. 

Anne closed her eyes. She was conscious of the proximity of this man realized that he possessed a subtle attraction that forced itself upon her at certain moments.

She sat in silence for a moment imagining Roger in London, crossing from his chambers in the Temple to the Law Courts, or taking a taxi to the grim, grey stone building known as the Old Bailey.

She was surprised to find herself feeling sorry for him, partly because of her own unworthiness to become his wife, partly because she had so obviously deceived him. 

She ought to have admitted from the first that, although she respected and admired him, she had never loved him. And then, after a while, he would have seen the fultility of it all and drifted away and Anne would have missed him a little and later become interested in someone else. It was the same type of thing over and over again, until Anne wanted to laugh at the absurdity of it all. Ultimately, one realized that, generally speaking, men were nearly all alike in the essential characteristics. 

When a woman fell in love, it usually turned out to be the small, unimportant characteristics that made her think him different to any other man . . . an oddly [ 46 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

acquired mannerism; a certain boyishness that appealed to the maternal instinct; the thousand and one affectations of speech and habit that a woman always observed and magnified to the exclusion of everything else. Fundamentally, almost every man on comparatively short acquaintance could be catalogued under one of three types. There was the man of the type to which Adolphe belonged: the man-about-town, well-dressed, possessing perfect savoir faire, who was a trifle unscrupulous; who read La Vie Parisienne and was a connoisseur of women's legs. There was the type to which men like Roger belonged: sports-loving, well-bred public school men, amongst whom there was a certain unwritten law concerning certain things that were "not done"; men who were practical and meticulously honest in all things; men who fell in love rather young, believing women to be goddesses, and never looked at another woman until the wife in question, bored with the dull routine of married life, eloped with a fashionable rotter, when they took rather heavily to drink and its accompanying vices, wrote a long letter about going abroad to start life afresh in the great open spaces (and usually got no further than their club where they drowned their sorrows in the whiskey bottle), and went about advertising their disillusion and cursing women in general. There was the third type, and the most objectionable: the man who had worked his way up in the world and married a woman a little better bred than himself. He was usually considered the "perfect husband," because he spent all his spare time telling people how he had worked his way up from comparative obscurity, and how he'd never had the time nor money to indulge in the wild dissipations of modern youth. He [ 47 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

came home regularly from the office; loaded his wife with jewels, because, he said, the world judged a man by his wife. The wife was usually a quiet, gentle little thing and not a bit fashionable; but she pleased him because she was always telling everybody how wonderful he was. 

This type of man was also rather a tyrant, and besides this, was also amazingly deceitful and nearly always indulged in a love affair with his typist, who took all she could get, called him "daddy" and thought him a "silly old fool."

Out and away! Through the water they darted like a torpedo in a mad burst of speed. The beach tilted and vanished over a thin blue line. There was a cloud of spray at the stern and the roar of the engine churned the glassy sea to sudden life. 

Half buried beneath the mackintosh, Anne felt the salt water dash over her face soaking her short hair and running into her eyes. The wind sang in her ears and deafened her. She called out to Rollo, half-choking as she gasped for breath, but the roar of the engine and the wind drowned her cry and made it but a faint little echo.

Each disappeared behind a boulder of rock and quickly undressed. A few moments later they were swimming out from the shore, Rollo with his dark head bent to the water and his brown arm cleaving the water with long, clean strokes; Anne using the side-stroke, so that only her scarlet cap and one gleaming wet shoulder was visible above water. "Back, now," cried Rollo turning shorewards again, and, laughing, Anne followed him. Up the shore they ran, two slim brown figures dripping with water, costumes clinging to their bodies, and flung themselves on the shingly beach, face upwards, in the sun. "This is Heaven," cried Anne, as she removed her cap and shook her hair. She cast a glance at Rollo beside her, noting his long lithe body and the muscles rippling [ 52 ]

WHITE PIERROT under the tanned skin. His eyes were closed . . . wet, dark lashes like a fan against each cheek. "Will you kiss me, me, Anne?" Her mind flew back to countless other men and their swift, furtive kisses; she had said and thought the same things then as she did now. People said that kisses meant nothing these days, but Anne knew differently.

It was not because she was narrow minded or more virtuous than other women, but simply because she had studied their cause and effect. Kisses, lightly indulged, developed into other things, more serious, and lulled one into a false emotion. You could almost judge a man by his kisses; they acted as a medium through which you plumbed the depths of his mind and although, of course, no two men kissed quite alike, this did not deceive girls like Anne who knew their world and yet remained untouched by its phases of vulgarity. Anne was glad that the intimate conversation between Rollo and herself had come to an abrupt end. She won- [55]

WHITE PIERROT

She supposed it to be the result of polygamous natures and a certain callousness. Anne believed in marriage, despite her promiscuity and despite her conviction that it spelt finality to herself. It brought happiness to the right kind of people who mixed common sense and judgment with their love.

She belted her green frock and stepped into the pool of sunshine on the other side of the rock.

About them there hung the smell of warm damp earth, whilst overhead the leafy branches of the trees made a soft lace pattern against the sky.

Anne was thinking, with fierce resentment: "Men, men, men life is bounded on all sides by them. CHAPTER VI 

T a few minutes past eleven that night Lillian and her party entered the Casino and made for one of the smaller rooms.

It was nearly twelve before Lillian, a careful but heavy backer, began to play, and Anne, having already won three hundred francs "en plein," stood behind her aunt's chair intent upon watching her play. 

The famous glass chandeliers, with their vivid, white lights, shone down upon the nightly clientele of fashionable gamblers. Jewels of every description flashed against the lavish evening clothes of the women and by their brilliance enhanced the more somber black and white of the men.

The room was heavy with tobacco smoke and the varied perfume of the women; for no breath of air was allowed to penetrate the heavy velvet curtains shrouding the windows. High up in the ceiling several enormous fans churned up the already humid air.

The crescendo of sound rose in accordance with the play of the wheel and even from the entrance doors you knew whether the ball had clicked into place by the sudden clamor of voices, the rustle of paper, the momentary hush that fell upon the gamblers as the wheel neared its final revolution. [64]

WHITE PIERROT 

There were the usual types sitting at the tables: the types one finds at every gaming table in the world. Anne watched them all as she stood behind her aunt's chair, and marvelled at the fact that in most of them, so obviously wealthy, there still lived the lust for money; that their only thrill in life seemed the chance of winning or losing a fortune in one turn of the wheel. 

There was a quiet little man, who sat beside the croupier with a pile of money and chips in front of him, backing heavily according to a plan perfected by himself. 

His face, pale and expressionless, only a slight twitching of the lips betraying the nervous tension he suffered. He occupied the same seat each night from the moment that the bank opened until the grey light of dawn signalled his departure. Near him was a fat woman, resplendent in jewels and an ermine cloak, who played foolishly and giggled like a schoolgirl. Behind her stood her indulgent husband, watching her with obvious amusement, saying, when she lost: "Never mind, dearie, there's more where that comes from. . ." or when she won: "That's right, my dear, you'll break the bank yet." Good humoredostentatious-the nouveau riche. Further along there sat the most pathetic figures, with white face and trembling hands, who watched their fortune dwindle, leaving them penniless-the real gamblers. When the last franc had been drawn away by the croupier, they rose wearily from the table and staggered out into the darkness. Man or woman, their Fate was sealed by the turn of the wheel.

Some drifted to obscurity, or caught the train back to their native town with the return half of a ticket. Others, more desperate, and unable to face the possibility of debt and dishonor, shot themselves in the gardens surrounding [65]

WHITE PIERROT 

the Casino, outside the very windows where Nemesis had overtaken them!

The atmosphere was unhealthy, artificial, depressing, because of the hint of tragedy that underlay the gaiety. 

Anne wished herself in the little speed-boat again, skimming over the calm sea with the roar of the wind in her ears, and a pathway of seething foam in the wake of the boat. Warm sunshine; the tang of salt water on her lips; the sudden silence as they glided through the water and came unexpectedly upon golden sands and the greasy slopes of the hills.

Suddenly Anne knew that she could bear it no longer. 

She leaned forward and whispered to her aunt, who nodded without lifting her eyes from the table. 

Down the steps, through the baccarat rooms and the crowded restaurant, and Anne found herself in the quiet gardens. Fairy lights everywhere, and the soft rustle of the night breeze in the tall cypress trees darkly silhouetted against the moonlit sky.

A stream of cars were lined against the main gates as Anne came into the broad drive that led to the promenade. Drawn up against a score of others she saw a blue and silver Hispaño Suiza from which four people had just alighted.

In desperation she took cover behind a clump of bushes, scarcely knowing why she did so, yet urgently aware that she must not be seen by Rollo.

During the last few days she had been acutely conscious of the almost constant supervision of Hugh Randall, so that she had begun to feel virtually a prisoner. She realized that Hugh feared her meeting Rollo again and had planned her last days so that there was no ordinary possi- [71]

WHITE PIERROT 

bility of such a thing happening. At first she had been amused, then a little resentful, and finally fierce rebellion welled up in her and overpowered her more acquiescent spirit.

After all, she argued to herself, why should she not wish Rollo good-bye? Having left Monte Carlo, she would never be likely to see him again. There was pain and unhappiness in this last thought, but Anne stifled it and reassured herself that it was all for the best. 

It was but the word of a minute to swing her legs over the low stone wall and drop into the low bushes on the other side. She repeated to herself "Villa Elegie" until the words burned like fire in her brain.

She lay back against the dusty cushions and realized for the first time the madness of it all... the hopelessness of such an adventure. She was foolish to have come all this way on the off chance of finding Rollo at home. She might have known that he would be out, somewhere, and would not return until late, if at all. 

She felt absurdly sleepy after her long journey, and the sun was pleasantly warm. ROGER

CHAPTER I 

 Ro 

OGER FORDYCE sat down to an early breakfast in the oak-panelled dining-room at No. 9 Harrington Gardens.

It was mid-June and the room was filled with pale sunlight that filtered in through the half-drawn curtains. 

Wide bay windows overlooked a pleasant lawn interspersed with neat beds of flowers and an old apple tree threw cool shadows against the southern wall and strove in vain to give lie to the fact that the garden terminated at this hideous juncture of bricks and mortar instead of continuing through an interminable vista of trees and flowers.

On this particular morning, however, Roger Fordyce was infinitely more interested in the pile of correspondence that lay beside his plate than in the beauty and tranquillity of the garden. Between mouthfuls of toast and coffee he perused each letter slowly and carefully, pausing now and again as if to weigh some delicate point at the back of his mind.

From the top of his thick fair hair to the tip of his well polished shoes, Roger Fordyce was the embodiment of care and refinement. His appearance was that of the well-groomed man who is fastidious as regards detail without being either effeminate or over-dressed. Tall and [83]

WHITE PIERROT 

broad-shouldered, with a long fair face and keen blue eyes, he had inherited from his father a certain impassivity of expression and intensity of purpose that had grown on him since, as an only son, the directorship of a large firm had forced him into responsibility at an early age. Since boyhood there had been instilled in Roger the firm conviction that the primary consideration in life was the honor and integrity of "Fordyce & Gaskell," a firm of lawyers whose name was a by-word in City circles and whose reputation had remained unblemished throughout the half century of its existence. 

From the time that he had come down from Oxford, in his early twenties, young Fordyce had worked hard and conscientiously. There had been long years of study in preparation for his examinations in law and it was not until he had reached the age of thirty-four that, his final examination passed with honors, he was at last admitted to the Bar. From that point onward, encouraged by a certain degree of success, he had devoted himself entirely to the interests of his firm and it was only at the termination of each session that he allowed himself the slightest relaxation. On these occasions, before the advent of 

Anne, he had played an indifferent game of golf or toured the lanes of Devonshire in his high-powered Bentley.

Since his engagement, however, there had been a rearrangement of his spare time and it had then been spent in the company of Anne and various friends of hers at night clubs and parties in which he had never been particularly interested but which he had attended solely to please her.

Since his very early school days Roger Fordyce had been a keen student of human nature, so that without [ 84 ]

WHITE PIERROT ever having been particularly interested in women he had always understood a good deal about them. This insight into character had been born in him and was further encouraged by his later training, it becoming part of his work to sum up the finer points of the characters of the various types of men and women with whom he came in contact. 

When the sudden death of Robert Chetwynd had brought Lillian to the offices of Fordyce & Gaskell to discuss certain legal matters, she had met Roger Fordyce and at once been attracted by him. She had invited him, with her usual gushing generosity, to dine at her Bruton

Street flat and meet certain of her friends, but Roger had politely refused. He had accepted a later invitation, however, having in mind that Robert Chetwynd had been a good client of the firm and that they owed his rather impossible widow a little courtesy on that account. 

Subsequently Roger Fordyce dined several times at the flat in Mayfair, hating it rather more each time but consoled by the vague interest he felt for Lillian's daughter, Pip. For a man so cold, so reserved and so versed in the minor tragedies of life, Roger was surprised at the swift sympathy he felt for this sad-eyed child whose pathetic story he had divined from the by-play of hurried glances and words that had passed between the girl and her mother.

The meeting of Anne, some months later, had been one of the landmarks in his life, an incident always vividly remembered. He had fallen in love with her slowly and rather reluctantly, like a cautious person groping in the dark, uncertain of himself and still less certain of Anne. From the first, he had found her an arresting [85]

WHITE PIERROT 

personality whose character possessed little subtleties that eluded him, so that he always thought of her as a longlimbed, tomboyish little person who said things in a queer, husky voice, things that, even if sometimes indiscreet, bore the hallmark of originality and were always interesting. He did not attempt to explain his love for her, that deep, overwhelming emotion that became allabsorbing and finally swamped all other interests in his life. He thanked God for it and looked upon its reciprocation as an undeserved blessing.

There had been a time, when Anne had gone away, that he had been afraid that the combined influence of 

Lillian and Monte Carlo would upset their engagement, for although he trusted Anne, he did not trust the insidious glamor of an environment which, because of its newness and novelty, might possibly lessen the importance of their love for each other. Then again he had been afraid that she might meet someone else, someone who would sweep her off her feet so that she would forget her love for him. For weeks the thought of that had been like a stab in the back, for he could not imagine his life, now, without her. Somehow she had grown into his very being and become part of his existence, so that when he lay awake at night and thought of her, miles and miles away, turning night into day and doing every damnable thing that he hated, he wanted to fling back the bed-clothes and take the first train down to her. 

He experienced no real jealousy in these wild moments; only a terrible fear that he would lose her, and the pain of such a thought became almost physical. Then, when daylight came and with it vague sleepiness and a sense of shame because of his absurd distrust, Roger would [86]

WHITE PIERROT 

fall asleep cursing the Westfield case that had kept him in court for five weeks and worn his nerves to mere rags. 

Just before Easter, Anne had returned to London. 

Roger had waited for the arrival of the Dover train at the noisy, smoky station, wondering how much longer the train would be and wondering, too, how Anne would look. And when the train finally drew into the platform and he had scanned the carriages from end to end, Anne suddenly appeared from nowhere. "Hello, Roger," she had said in her cool, clear voice.

He had been so overjoyed at the sight of her that he had stood a moment, looking at her without speaking, and then, like an awkward schoolboy, he had bent and kissed her lips. "How suburban of you," Anne had laughed.

They had been joined by Lillian, Adolphe and various other members of their party and further private conversation had been impossible. "Dinner, to-night, Anne?" Roger had asked, as she alighted from a taxi with a pile of luggage and stood on the doorstep of her Chelsea flat looking back at him. 

She had smiled assent and, driving back to the West End, Roger had thought vaguely: "Her eyes are different . . . she looks tired and older." ...

They had dined together that night in the cool diningroom of the Carlton and Roger had discussed his business, their approaching marriage and the house in Harrington Gardens. "You'll like it, Anne," he had murmured eagerly, remembering hours and hours spent with decorators and carpenters, supervising the intricacies of interior decoration. [ 87 ]

WHITE 

PIERROT "I'd like to see the house, Roger," Anne had said at last. "To-morrow, dear." "No, I want to see it to-night . . . there's time."

Amused at her whim, he raised no further protest and a few minutes later they had jumped into the Bentley and run down to South Kensington.

Tall and gaunt, the dark house loomed up at the corner of a quiet square and flung long shadows against the moonlit pavement.

They had wandered through the empty house, from room to room, still fresh from the smell of paint and plaster, and Anne had loved it all. Together they had stood in the doorways of the rooms and planned where certain pieces of furniture should go and decided the colors of carpets and curtains. 

Later, they had driven back to the West End and danced at the Embassy Club until the small grey hours of dawn.

The next few weeks had been devoted to hours and hours of shopping; to arranging the furniture in the new house; to ordering Anne's trousseau; to fixing up final and complicated details of the wedding, and during this time Roger saw very little of Anne. He cursed volubly all the thousand and one appointments that kept her running to and fro from morning till night, making her gradually more pale and tired. 

The day of the wedding, with all its pomp and ceremony, had arrived at last, followed by an important function when everybody made foolish speeches and drank a good deal too much champagne. Roger had longed to get away, hating as he did the crowds and [88]

WHITE PIERROT noise and the inane laughter, but Anne had appeared to enjoy it all. Curiously enough, he remembered more clearly than anything else the pale little face of Pip Chetwynd outlined against a high-backed chair and two dark eyes that had sought his across a veritable sea of faces. And some moments later a white, fluttering hand had plucked his sleeve as he passed. "I hope you'll be happy, Roger." Conventional words, yet, spoken by Pip in her tremulous little voice, they held a greater significance and Roger had looked down into her uplifted face and smiled. "Thank you, Pip. You must come and see us as soon as we get back."

A few days' honeymoon in Paris, wonderful days with Anne to himself at last, and then back to the turmoil of London and the grey precincts of the law courts. 

Roger's marriage to Anne had fired him with renewed ambition and immediately upon his return to London he made fresh plans for the advancement of his career and the enlargement of the interests of his firm. In the months that followed, the routine of "Fordyce & Gaskell" underwent several changes and business opened out on a larger scale.

On this June morning in question, however, Roger had received a disturbing letter from his father, who, despite advanced years and failing health, kept an interest in the firm through the weekly letters of his son. 

It was nearly three months since Roger and Anne had taken up residence at Harrington Gardens, and their first dinner party, to be given for the purpose of breaking the ice and formally opening the house to their more intimate [89]

WHITE PIERROT 

friends, had been fixed for that particular night. The letter which Roger had just received, in which his father took objection to the inclusion of criminal cases by the firm, had somewhat upset him and banished from his mind all thoughts of the impending dinner party. 

He drank the last drops of his coffee and, picking up the small pile of correspondence, went up to the room where Anne lay drowsily in a low divan bed. 

Looking down at her for a moment, small, white and golden as she lay curled up between palest mauve sheets, he restrained a fleeting impulse to kiss her and instead seated himself heavily on the end of the bed, placing a wide expanse of mauve satin eiderdown between them.

Anne said sleepily, opening large hazel eyes and appraising Roger's dark, well-cut suit: "Anything the matter, darling?" "I've had a letter from Father, refusing to allow me to touch criminal cases on behalf of the firm. I'm pretty wild about it, Anne. The firm's been stuck in a rut for years because of Father's obstinacy, and after my successful handling of the Westfield case I thought he'd come round. It means I shall have to run down and see him." 

Anne sat up in bed and leaned on one elbow, surveying 

Roger with faint amusement. "You take everything so seriously, Roger. Look here, your father left the business to you, so I don't see how he can interfere with your management of it." "But I couldn't take a big step like that without consulting him. It's out of the question, especially as he's been dead set against criminal work for years.'

-2- At eight o'clock that evening Roger and Anne stood in the lounge at the foot of the wide oak staircase and received their guests.

A good number of people had already arrived and the lounge seemed almost full. The evening gowns of the women glistened against the somber background of old oak and crimson velour and diamonds sparkled in the subdued amber light. There were low voices, laughter, the smell of expensive cigarettes, the tinkle of cocktail glasses. 

And dinner came at nine o'clock, with Roger and Anne in their respective places at the two ends of the long narrow table. Butlers moved forward with one accord; there came the clink of ice as champagne was removed from the buckets; the swift flow of conversation as each guest discovered, as if by magic, he or she sat beside the one person he or she would have chosen as a neighbor. 

Across the banks of flowers, Roger caught a momentary glimpse of Anne's face, flushed and smiling as she bent towards her right-hand guest, chatting gaily.

Halfway down the table, Lillian amused some three or four people by her recitation of certain mythical adventures that had befallen her while staying at her villa on the Riviera. Almost opposite her, Pip sat engrossed in the lively conversation of Howard Trent, a young man who had been up at Oxford with Roger and was now a flourishing newspaper editor. Looking at these two, Roger thought that they made an excellent pair . . . he wondered whether they would find each other attractive he hoped so, and then he remembered something .. · [94]

WHITE PIERROT 

Lillian had said, months ago, and which annoyed him intensely at the time. "Men don't like Pip. She's dull.”

Roger had thought Pip quiet and reserved, but never dull. Even her long silences had never caused him any embarrassment, knowing as he did that they disguised a glowing, inward happiness that was all the more eloquent because it could not be expressed in mere words. 

There was a day, months ago, when he had taken her to a recital at the Albert Hall. He had thought her very quiet on this occasion, yet looking at her sideways during the performance he had seen her dark head bent forward over the edge of their box and her eyes shining, gleaming onyx set in the pale ivory of her face . . . and in her lap two little clenched hands that showed the knuckles white against the darkness of her frock.

Then suddenly she had swayed towards him and he had seen her lips pale and trembling and her eyes closed, as she thrust out a limp hand to find him. "I think I'm ill, Roger," she had whispered.

He had caught her up in his arms, a limp, tired child whose soft, dark hair brushed across his face as he carried her down the echoing stone stairs and dingy corridors to the waiting car below. A wave of tenderness had come over him, so that he had held her close and touched her cold forehead with his lips. "Poor child,” he had thought as he forced brandy between her white lips from a flask that he kept in the car. 

She had opened her eyes and smiled at him, a weak little smile, because a pain in her side stabbed like a knife and caught her breath. "I'm so sorry, Roger," she had whispered, pressing one hand to her side. [ 95 ]

Pip might know real happiness; he decided there and then to discuss the matter with Anne to see if they could do anything to help things along. He began to be amused, laughed to himself as he played with the thought of being a "matchmaker." It was usually the women who adopted this rôle.

Towards the end of dinner, several friends, warmed by the pleasant effect of too much champagne, thought it their duty to say a few words about their charming host and hostess; the excellent dinner; the beautiful house which they had the pleasure of visiting for the first time, etc.

WHITE PIERROT Very small and childish she looked, as she perched herself on the high stool and fluttered thin fingers over the white keys. And beside her, his pleasant face wreathed in smiles, stood Howard Trent. From his low seat by the window Roger observed the flicker of interest in his brown eyes as he fixed them intently upon Pip's bent head, and suddenly he knew he was glad. 

Silence fell upon the assembly as the first notes of the Sonata flew across the room, sighed, swayed, hung on a single note and died away in a minor key. There seemed no real melody in this strange composition; no definite arrangement of chords and time; no repetition of any sort; but the swift, fluttering music hung like a spell over the listening audience and thrilled them because of its lightness, its whimsicality, its subtle daring. And when at length Pip lifted her fingers from the last quivering notes, a sudden hush enveloped the room and thus paid her a greater tribute than the loudest applause. 

From a corner by the window Roger Fordyce ran the tip of his tongue over lips gone suddenly dry. His eyes were fixed upon two small hands that rested limply over the edge of the keyboard. He dared not lift his eyes and meet the dark ones that spanned the distance between them, as intent upon him as if he were the only person in the room who mattered; his glance travelled past her and caught, instead, the rapt expression on the face of Howard Trent, as the latter bent towards her and whispered something in her ear. And then he saw Pip lift her head and smile into the keen, young face above her; saw her dark eyes, surprised, as if she had been unaware that the young man had stood beside her from the first moment she began to play. WHITE PIERROT 

A tumult of applause followed the momentary silence and Pip was immediately surrounded by a crowd of people, all anxious to praise her for her exquisite composition.

He started, awakened out of his reverie, and looked down at her. "Nothing, darling, you are always imagining things..."

Roger shook his head and then Anne remembered the little incident of the morning. "It's about Pip, Roger. You are annoyed about her playing for us?" "No, I'm not, Anne. She told me herself that she wanted to play."

The guests were dispersing now, some wandering into the ballroom, others to the bridge tables, and a few moved in chequered shadow on the wide stone terrace that led down to the quiet garden.

WHITE PIERROT 

stood in silence-Anne, pink and white and golden against the slim blackness of Roger's coat, looking up into his white, set face and thinking to herself that he seemed like a stranger to her, a tall handsome stranger whom she didn't understand and who seemed suddenly miles and miles away from her. 

Across the lounge there drifted the soft rhythm of a fox-trot, the faint shuffle of feet, the light sound of voices raised in laughter. And Anne was back again in another world; in a darkened ballroom, dancing amongst a thousand shadowy figures, with only a bright gleam of mingled red and purple light throwing into relief the vague outline of a dark head above her; and through the shuffling of feet and the throbbing of music a voice had said: "I love you." Music-lights-laughter-carnival madness. She and a white pierrot in a fool's paradise! 

And it had all been a dream—a sweet dream, that made the memory of it hurt, a dream too swift, too bright, too unreal.

Here in this conventional London drawing-room, with Roger beside her, she faced cold, stark reality. Nothing in the past mattered. The two of them would go through life bound together by the sanctity of marriage and the four walls of their home.

The music stopped, a voice called her and Anne came back to earth with a half smothered sigh. She turned and without another word hurried into the ballroom. 

Surprised because the long silence between them remained unexplained, Roger pulled his tie straight and followed her.

It was after two o'clock when the last car slipped away and left the quiet square deserted. [99]

WHITE PIERROT 

Anne went slowly up to her room and, behind her, a single amber light glowed in the darkness and threw her distorted reflection across the panelled walls.

The night air seemed very warm and the stillness oppressive, so that Anne flung open a window overlooking the dim shadowed square and stood leaning out into the darkness.

WHITE PIERROT 

his father lived. At all costs he must see his father and get this new business settled. It meant a good deal to his future.

He crept into bed some half hour later, fearful of disturbing Anne, whose light, regular breathing came to him through the darkness. 

He lay awake for a long time, tired, yet unable to sleep. 

He watched the faint breeze rustling the curtains and saw the first drops of rain fall and hang like great white diamonds on the windows. Low, black clouds rolled across the sky and obscured the thin white arc of the moon.

And through the windswept clouds and the moon and the slow, heavy drops of rain, he saw the pale face of Pip, like a mirage, and the masses of her hair that melted into the blackness of the clouds. ... ... 

Pip and Howard Trent! At dinner and through the evening he had hoped hoped desperately . . . hoped for her sake . . . that . . . And then he knew that it didn't matter now; knew that even if Howard Trent loved her in time to come, it could make no difference. 

It was all too late! Pip loved him, Roger Fordyce!

He suddenly hated the quality in him that made people's hearts and minds an open book to him; hated it because now and always he must know Pip's secret . . . must know that, whatever happened, complete happiness for her was out of the question. It seemed that in one way or another she must always suffer. What cruel, freakish tricks Fate played upon people and laughed, like some hideous devil, when they hurt!

Pip loved him, but he, Roger, loved Anne and was [ IOI ]

WHITE PIERROT 

married to her! Oh, God, what a ghastly muddle life was!

He turned over, stiff and cold, unable to sleep because memories of Pip haunted him. He moved and felt 

Anne's warm little body against him; heard her halfwaking cry as he disturbed her by his restlessness. 

And ever since he had known Pip, in those early days when he had dined at the flat in Bruton Street, he had prayed for her happiness; prayed that some earthly miracle would banish the sadness from those big, dark eyes. 

He had felt for her a compassion that had steadily grown out of his pity for her, and by her very helplessness she had always appealed to the chivalry in his nature. And now everything had gone wrong. Even the advent of 

Howard Trent would make no difference. Pip, because of her love for him, would never know real happiness!

The thought cut through him like a knife as he lay there staring into an abyss of darkness. [102]

CHAPTER II 

HE following morning Roger had left for Devonshire long before Anne was awake. 

It was a warm, bright day and Anne rose earlier than usual and breakfasted downstairs.

Having given her orders to the cook and supervised certain domestic affairs, she wandered over the house, from room to room, feeling a sudden keen pleasure in the knowledge that this was her own home and that all the treasures in each room belonged to her. She had experienced great joy in the buying and arranging of the furniture and in the blending of a color scheme throughout the house.

Each room bore the hallmark of personal interest; each room satisfied the eye because of its richness, its color, and its scrupulous attention to detail. There was no sign of the custom practiced by certain wealthy people who gave a carte blanche order to Warings or Maples to furnish throughout for an agreed sum of money. 

Anne was writing in Roger's study when the telephone bell rang. The butler came to her and announced that 

Mrs. Chetwynd desired to speak to her. She laid down her pen and hurried into the lounge, where the 'phone stood beneath its stiff silk cover. "That you, Anne?" [103]

They finished lunch and went on to discuss the success of Anne's party the previous night, but the latter was worried and preoccupied. She was thinking that Pip's future outlook was decidedly uncertain unless she married someone who could comfortably take care of her. She wondered if Pip knew quite how serious the position was and how much depended upon her early venture into matrimony.

It was three o'clock when Anne left Bruton Street and walked back to Knightsbridge via Park Lane.

One knew that every girl, in her heart, looked forward to marriage at some time in her life and it was only false pride and the realization of lost opportunities that prompted the absurd excuse: "I hate men" or "I don't want to marry." Anne wended her way home and changed into tennis kit ready for a game with Mrs. Evan James who had returned with them from the Riviera.

WHITE PIERROT 

Anne laughed. The sharp sound of it jarred Roger, so that he turned away and looked out into the shadowed square. Above the crowd he saw a solitary head raised and a pale face turned towards him and two dark eyes that sought his ... wistful eyes, which in their very dumb eloquence betrayed a secret locked away in a sad little heart. "No I don't think she ever will," he said at last, in a hoarse whisper. Anne had not heard him and he was glad for she had fallen asleep whilst he had been dreaming.

Down the dark stairs he crept again, feeling his way and switching on the amber-colored light in the lounge. 

He sat down before a tray that held cold chicken, salad, and coffee in a silver pot that percolated slowly over a small spirit stove. "Oh, Pip, this means worse than Hell for you. You must marry Howard Trent. D'you understand, Pip? 

I'll try not to see you again—perhaps that'll help. You must forget that I ever existed."

But somehow he knew that she wouldn't; knew that, despite her youth, Pip was the kind of girl, loyal and sincere, who loved only once in a lifetime, and forever.

The soft amber light gleamed at him like a large yellow eye through the darkness. Sitting there, cold and tired, eating a meal that he scarcely tasted, Roger wondered desperately how it would all end.

He crept up to his bedroom again and slept heavily until morning. [116]

White lights-beneath them a moving stream of people; music that rose above the crescendo of voices and died away as nine o'clock chimed from the distant church; and over all an air of expectancy that increased with the passing of each slow minute. 

The house seemed full to its utmost capacity and the waiting audience, almost as one person, seized their programs and studied them with interest, offering careless comment upon certain details that interested them. The front page was adorned with the cameo portrait of a young, dark-haired girl whose unhappy expression brought forth caustic remarks from a certain section of the house.

In one of the boxes Lillian and her party sat behind half-drawn curtains and occasionally the door was flung open to admit the tall, ascetic figure of Professor Ames [122]

WHITE PIERROT 

who seemed to be laboring under the tension of the moment and was gesticulating wildly with every word he uttered. "It's a good thing that we have another artist on the program to help the evening along, because Miss Chetwynd is already in a very nervous state," he said.

WHITE PIERROT 

warmth. A violin case lay across the top of the piano and almost as soon as the curtains fell apart, an elderly man took his place at the piano and Monsieur Beauclert appeared from the opposite wing. Short, grey-haired and dressed in a badly fitting dinner jacket, he bowed gravely to the audience and announced, in swift French, his first number.

In their box Lillian and her party fidgeted uneasily and showed a very perfunctory interest in the artistic efforts of the little Frenchman, although he was actually an exceptionally fine violinist.

His first number, a prolonged classic by Schumann, lasted nearly forty minutes and the audience became increasingly restless. When the last notes died away, the burst of applause was not unmixed with a certain relief and, as the curtains were lowered for a short interval, the babble of voices rose again and programs were perused for enlightenment as to the following number. 

Professor Ames then appeared on the stage and a wave of excitement hushed the audience into momentary silence. In a nervous and husky voice the Professor announced that his pupil, Miss Phillipa Chetwynd, was an entirely unknown artist of only twenty years of age, who was giving her first recital this evening. He hoped they would be kind enough to excuse any nervousness on her part, as, naturally, her first public appearance would prove rather an ordeal; but he would hasten to assure them that this young lady was a wonderful artist and would, he hoped, become one of the finest pianists in the world. He would now have great pleasure in introducing Miss Chetwynd.

Excitement had now reached its zenith and, as Pip took her seat at the piano and adjusted herself with swift, nervous movements, applause filled the house and lasted for several minutes. Beethoven and Liszt. She played extremely well and after the first few bars did not appear to be at all nervous or distressed. WHITE PIERROT 

There came another pause whilst Pip seemed to have forgotten the sequence of the bars, and the next moment, to the astonishment of everybody, a new rhythm had taken the place of the old, and unfamiliar music fell upon the surprised ears of the audience and sent a little murmur of consternation amongst it. Lillian and Adolphe, with Anne and the professor in their wake, followed him into the room and closed the door against the curious eyes of press men and theater officials. 

A doctor appeared upon the scene almost at once, explaining that he had been amongst the audience and had observed Miss Chetwynd's distress.

She stood there a moment, gazing vacantly at the sofa where Pip had lain a short time ago; then she turned and went from the room, drawing her silk coat closer about her. And in her heart a voice cried, dully and pitifully: "Oh, Rollo-why did I ever leave you?"

The dim stone corridors echoed her stumbling footsteps and out in the crowded street below the world flowed past her, a world full of lights and laughter a deluded world; a vast audience amused by the puppets of life, the clowns, the playthings of Fate. She stood alone at the side of the curb outside the [129]

WHITE PIERROT 

darkened hall, looking for Roger and the Bentley. A burly commissionaire came up to her and touched his cap. "Mrs. Fordyce?" he asked politely and Anne nodded. "Mr. Fordyce asked me to put you in a taxi, madam, as he was obliged to take the young lady home without delay."

A taxi drew up as he spoke and Anne stepped into it and was driven slowly down to South Kensington. 

The house was in darkness when she arrived, but she turned on the lights in the lounge and flung herself into the nearest chair. She felt numb, somehow, almost as if the blood had frozen in her veins whilst the rest of her body lived.

Presently she got up and poured herself out a strong brandy and soda, which she gulped down with an expression of acute distaste; but the strong alcohol warmed her and sent the blood flowing through her again. She sat there for a while, looking at the various familiar objects about her and admiring, almost unconsciously, the luxurious furnishings-the richness of crimson carpet and hangings whose beauty was heightened by the subdued reflection of amber lights.

Half an hour passed and still Roger had not returned. 

Anne went slowly up to bed and undressed. She lay wide awake between the soft, cool sheets, listening intently for the familiar sound of the Bentley in the quiet square below. She wondered vaguely what had detained 

Roger for so long at Lillian's flat. Perhaps she ought to have 'phoned to ask if Pip had now fully recovered, but somehow she had expected Roger to appear with news at any moment. [130]

CHAPTER IV 

HERE is one type of man to whom Fate is most unkind: he is the unsophisticated but well-meaning husband who, fired with the ambition to make life more comfortable and pleasant for his wife, insists upon working about twice as hard as most other men and earning about twice as much money as the other less fortunate but wiser men, who return regularly from the office each day and forget their job until the next morning. There is a famous Biblical quotation to the effect: "What shall it profit a man if he gain the whole world and lose his own soul?" And, metaphorically, these words apply to that overworked, ambitious individual who, luxuriously feathering his nest by sheer hard work, loses, in the interim, the love and interest of his wife, and thus making his ultimate success very doubtful from the point of view of the marital happiness he had one day hoped to enjoy.

All women are vain in one respect-in that they will suffer anything but indifference and neglect. However unintentional this neglect may be on the part of the husband, it is to a woman the one unforgivable sin and there is no excuse that will compensate her for the bitter blow to her love and her vanity. If she is a proud woman, she will not bicker and quarrel when she observes signs of her husband's preoccupation, but will accept his indifference with hurt resignation, devoting her own [136]

WHITE PIERROT 

interest, meanwhile, to other things in an effort to lessen her disappointment.

In this way there develops the first breach, for the husband, misunderstanding his wife's attitude, accuses her of lack of interest in him and his affairs and cites her friends and the various outside interests she has adopted as the reason for it. Naturally argument is rather futile; for by the time the man has realized the true state of affairs, the barrier between them is almost insuperable and they are like two strangers speaking different languages. Subsequently they become increasingly antagonistic towards each other and if they continue to live together it is mainly because they fear the scandal of separation or divorce or because of their mutual interest in their children.

There is something very tragic about a marriage that is wrecked like this, because it is always unavoidable and only thoughtlessness and selfishness are the cause of it. 

Therefore, two people go through life, with the luxury about them which was so dearly bought, both lonely and unhappy; for despite their outside interests, nothing superficial can take the place of the intimacy and companionship that once existed between them. 

And so, two months later, we find Roger and Anne passing through the first phase of marital discord. 

It came upon them so gradually that it seemed almost imperceptible, and then a certain, definite incident awoke them to the realization of its presence. 

During the past two months the routine of married life at Harrington Gardens had undergone a subtle change, due in the first place to Roger's new work, and [137]

WHITE PIERROT 

later aggravated by Anne's attitude of resentment towards him.

Roger, with the pride and obstinacy that characterized the Fordyces, had opened his new offices in the Inner Temple and set about laying the foundations of his new career. It meant a hard fight from the first, but 

Roger had a good name and plenty of pluck to help him, so that, until a certain number of cases had given him the necessary publicity and inspired people's confidence in him, he had devoted himself to the studying of a branch of the law that was new to him. 

He had with him, as his right hand man, an old clerk from the firm of "Fordyce & Gaskell" who had begged to go along with Roger and sink or swim with the new enterprise. "You see, sir, I'm in a rut here-can't get a move up for years, SO if you'll have me I'd rather take my chance with you."

Thus, in this way, Harry Danvers became Roger's confidential secretary and the two of them worked each night until eleven and twelve chimed from St. Paul's, when they would look up from their desks, appear surprised at the lateness of the hour and then wend their opposite ways homeward.

And then came the day when Sir David Moss, bearing in mind Roger's masterly handling of the Westfield case six months ago, passed on to him his first important brief. 

This was a big event in Roger's life and would make or mar his future, but it was an opportunity that he had worked and waited for and now that it had come he was going to make the most of it. The night that Roger received this brief for the Carter case, he and Danvers [138]

WHITE PIERROT worked until past midnight, for there was a great deal to be arranged in a short time and Roger determined there would be no hitch.

It was late when he returned home that night, looking unusually pale and tired. It was weeks since he had dined at home, having eaten sandwiches at the office or taken scrappy meals at a nearby restaurant in Fleet Street. 

But nothing mattered to him now that his reward had come and he entered the quiet house in Harrington Gardens pleased and happy because success was within his reach.

He found Anne waiting for him in his study. She wore a beautiful evening frock that was partially covered by a rich gold and sable coat. He bent and kissed her, expressing surprise at finding her there and flinging himself wearily into the nearest chair facing her. And then he noticed her face. It wore a cold, contemptuous expression such as he had never seen upon it before. He said, gently: "Is anything the matter, Anne?" "Yes," replied Anne in a dull voice. "That is why I have waited up for you. Do you remember your promise to me this morning?"

Roger sighed despondently and frowned at the shadow on the carpet. He was thinking, with inward triumph: "Anne is cross now, but wait till I tell her the wonderful news. And suddenly she hated Roger and his business, the importance of which was of more interest to him than her happiness. As plainly as she knew how, she had pointed out the danger line to Roger in their foolish quarrel the previous night; it wasn't as if she had let him go on, uncaring, unthinking, unrealizing. He had promised her, then, that in future things would be different; had promised her that he would not leave her so much, that he would faithfully attend all important social functions. hour. It all seemed to Anne so selfish, so thoughtless, so unnecessary, so much as if she were subordinate to his veriest whim, and also it seemed as if he took her very much for granted. gnarled elm trees that stood, sentinel-like, in the quiet garden below. 

And Anne remembered other nights, long ago, when the moon had watched over her and smiled from its cradle of clouds; smiled in a way that seemed so old and wise, as if it knew all the secrets of the world and was amused.

There had been that night when, from the garden of the villa, she had stood alone, looking upward at its silver face across the grey sea, and she had whispered of her loneliness, of her vague longing for adventure. And it had smiled, just as it always smiled, placidly and tolerantly, as if looking down upon a struggling world from its lofty height made everything below seem small and petty; as if, too, it wanted to say that the only great things in the world were the mighty elements . . .. the powers of Nature . . . the hand of Death that ended life's earthly struggles . . the will of God. But to 

Anne, that night, the greatest thing in the world had been the promise of adventure, and when at last adventure had come to her in the shape of a mysterious white pierrot, she had looked up and thanked it, as if, like a wizard, it had granted her wish. The moon had shone across the sea that night, turning to silver the bedraggled remnants of the carnival . . . the dusty confetti . . . the crushed, broken flowers. the garish costumes of homeward-bound carnival makers. ...

There had been another night, too, but she hardly dared think of it; yet the moon had still smiled, because her adventure with the white pierrot seemed such a little thing in a very crowded world and it had seemed to say: "Don't be afraid. Don't be afraid. I can keep your [154]

Since then she had seen the moon very seldom; sometimes it peeped at her through the curtains in her room when she lay awake; sometimes she saw it shining over roof-tops and streets and quiet squares; other times it mixed its silver sheen with the lurid gold of the electric lights in Piccadilly, night-times, after the theater, as she and Roger came home in the Bentley. CHAPTER V 

 T WAS early evening two days later, and Pip sat alone in the drawing-room of her mother's flat, writing a letter to Howard Trent.

It appeared to be a somewhat difficult letter to write, for the waste-paper basket overflowed with pieces of screwed up paper upon which only a few words had been written, and the end of her pen gave evidence, too, of having been persistently worried by the incision of some very sharp teeth. “Oh, dear, I must get this written before Lillian comes in," Pip murmured, frowning upon her latest effort. 

And then, apparently satisfied, she put her flourishing signature to it and was reading it over for the last time. 

It ran somewhat like this: 

"DEAR HOWARD: 

"I think I am writing this letter because I am too much of a coward to tell you face to face the things that, sooner or later, you will have to know. "You have been a wonderful friend to me and ever since I first met you at Roger's house you have always made me very happy. Now, however, you have asked me to marry you and go with you to your new post in Capetown, so I realize that it is only fair to let you know how I feel about things. [ 160 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

"I hate to hurt you, Howard, and I am terribly sorry to have to disappoint you, but it is quite out of the question for me to consider marrying you.

I never meant to encourage you by accepting your kind attentions, but I was so afraid to lose a very precious friend, almost the only friend I have ever had; but now I cannot hang back any longer and must be honest with you. "There are two reasons why I cannot marry you. 

One is very simple, I do not love you. The other is because I do not think it fair for me to marry any man owing to my very poor health. As you know 

I am subject to very severe heart trouble and am continually under treatment. It is not right to marry under such conditions, you will agree. "I can't expect you to remain my friend after this, but I do hope you will forgive me if I have ever allowed you to entertain any false hopes. Don't imagine that I am dismissing you lightly, because, as I have told you many times, my life is not a happy one and I realize that if I could have loved you, everything would have been different. "Good-bye, Howard dear, and please don't hate me for writing this, will you? It hurts, dreadfully. "Sincerely, "PIP." 

As Pip sealed the letter the telephone bell rang and, lifting off the receiver, she heard her mother's sharp voice over the wires. "I'm going straight on to dine at my club. Tell [161]

WHI HIT TE PIERROT Adolphe, will you, and ask him to call for me about eleven." "Very well, Mother," replied Pip, and hung up the receiver again.

The letter addressed, she went out into the hall and gave it to a maid to post; then returning to the drawingroom, she curled up in one of the big chairs and began to read the latest report of the Carter case. She had been delighted to learn that Roger had been handed the defense and had carefully read every paper in order to keep up with the latest reports. Occasionally she telephoned to Danvers, his secretary, to ask the latest news. 

The accused woman, Mrs. Henry Carter, seemed to have aroused widespread public sympathy on account of the extenuating circumstances of the case. It was alleged that she had deliberately poisoned her husband, a gardener, by administering several doses of arsenic in his food. Investigation had brought to light the fact that she had endured a life of torture and misery since her marriage twenty years ago, and that she had only continued living with him on account of her children. The accused denied having administered poison to her husband, but gave out that he had suicidal tendencies and when under the influence of drink had several times tried to end his life as well as that of his wife and children. 

She asserted firmly that there was no poison of any description in her house and that she had never bought any.

The case had been set back several times for further inquiries, and public opinion veered in favor of the accused woman whose life history began to fill the daily papers with its sordid details. [162]

WHITE PIERROT 

It was a big case and a difficult one for Roger, although he had worked extremely hard on it and was confident that, despite the strong case outlined by the prosecution, he would effect an acquittal. 

Pip had prayed nightly for his success, knowing that this case might set the seal upon his career, and she had determined that somehow or other she would get into court on the day that Roger first opened his speech for the defense.

As Pip was reading, the door opened and Adolphe came in. She looked up, surprised to see him, since he had stated his intention of dining out that night with a business acquaintance. She was a little annoyed, too, for she always avoided being alone with Adolphe whenever possible, and would have gone out for this evening had she known he would return so early. She repeated 

Lillian's message and relapsed once more into silence. 

Somehow she had always hated this swarthy Frenchman ever since her mother had brought him home from Monte Carlo. There was something about him that was veiled and sinister and she was acutely conscious of being a little afraid of him. He had a very dark skin, thin, black hair and sharp, narrow eyes. He was short, too, and had a slim figure that was always a trifle too elegantly waisted. Pip disliked, mostly, the aura of perfume about him that was a mixture of bath salts and scented hair-oil, and cringed, involuntarily, from the briefest touch of his hands, so cold, they seemed, and bloodless. 

He sat there now on the high piano stool, swinging his feet and watching her as she bent over her newspaper. 

Pip felt his eyes upon her; knew that they travelled slowly [163]

WHITE PIERROT over her and rested rather longer than was necessary upon her long, slim legs. "Pip . . .?"

She looked up coolly and surveyed him, thinking to herself that if Lillian saw him now, with that queer expression in his eyes, she would become mad with anger and jealousy. Lately this expression had appeared more often in his eyes and although Pip was always aware of it, she tried desperately to assign it to her imagination, told herself that Adolphe was of a particularly hateful type and, having thus catalogued him, avoided him as much as possible.

She rose to her feet now and pulled her short frock closer about her legs. "I'm going out now, Adolphe. You won't forget to call for Lillian, will you?" 99 She turned towards the door, but Adolphe recalled her. “Just one moment, little Pip . "Well...?" ...

He sat staring at her from the stool and a faint smile played about his lips. "Why is it, Pip, that you must avoid me so . . . as you do? What is it that I have done?" "I'm sorry, Adolphe, I haven't meant to," lied Pip, averting her head and fidgeting uneasily beside the halfopen door. "Ah, well, perhaps it is my imagination."

Adolphe rose to his feet and came slowly towards her. 

He said, gently: "I am your friend, child, you know that.." "Thank you, Adolphe," said Pip, nervously. [ 164 ]

WHITE PIERROT

"Thank you."
