It was nearly seven o'clock and she had not yet dined, having intended to indulge in one of her solitary meals on a tray in the drawing-room when the arrival of 

Adolphe had upset her plans.

The July evening was warm and Pip walked slowly, and by devious routes, toward Piccadilly. The streets and pavements were crowded and, wandering along, Pip realized for the first time how lonely one could feel amongst this hurrying stream of humanity.

There were three hours to while away somehow and 

Pip wondered what she could do and where she could go.

She did not feel in the mood for pictures, so she suddenly decided to take a bus down to South Kensington and visit Anne. If the latter happened to have gone out, as was most probable, then she would return home and go to bed.

It was curious that Pip had become a little disappointed in Anne since her marriage; for whereas she had imag- [165]

WHITE PIERROT 

ined her cousin making such a perfect wife, it seemed as if she were about to develop along the lines of a typical society hostess, with no thoughts above the entertainment and adulation of her friends, and little interest in her husband or his career. In a girl like Anne, so wise and so sincere, it was an amazing change and Pip could find nothing to account for it; for it seemed to her, that, of all the men in the world, there could hardly exist a more perfect husband than Roger Fordyce. To Pip, in her youth and inexperience, he was a king amongst men, who deserved happiness and the best of everything in life. 

Sometimes she felt that he had been cheated and it made her angry with Anne because Anne did not appear to care. 

Having arrived at No. 9 Harrington Gardens, Pip asked to see Mrs. Fordyce and was informed by the butler that only Mr. Fordyce was at home that evening.

Very soon I shall come back to you again, and then we shall make our plans . . . ... Yes?” 

Anne nodded dumbly and dried her eyes. She was suddenly ashamed of her lack of self-control, and without another word she stepped from the car and flew like a wild thing along the deserted street. And behind her she heard a voice calling her, begging a last kind word a last farewell, but the sound of it died away as she slipped into the quiet square and came upon a tall house standing gauntly on its shadowy corner. Her key made a little scraping noise as it turned in the lock. The lounge was in darkness, but for a single gleam of light that shone beneath the door of the study. Anne stood looking at it a moment, wondering if Roger had heard her enter the house; wondering, too, if he would pause in his work to come out and speak to her. No sound came from the little room, so Anne turned away and crept slowly up the stairs. There was a possibility that he had fallen asleep over his work, but she decided not to disturb him.

They had seen very little of each other lately, for each was hurt at the indifference of the other; both waited for a kind word or a smile, but pride was the ruling factor of their lives, so they waited in vain and meanwhile the gods laughed because two people were set at cross purposes and were heading for disaster. There is surely nothing in the world more cruel than the anger and bitterness between two women who fight over a man. CHAPTER VI 

 R°

OGER returned home about eight o'clock the following evening, having been detained after the closing of the court by certain business needing immediate attention.

He found a message awaiting him from Anne, to the effect that she had dined early and driven with a friend down to a little place on the river where she had hired a bungalow for the summer months.

He was a little disappointed, for he had rather hoped to find her at home; there were certain things he was anxious to say to her. During the last few weeks he had become aware of a tension in the atmosphere that had begun to prey on his nerves. To-night he felt as if he were leaning over the brink of a crisis. . . giddy and bewildered and vaguely afraid. There were other nights when he had felt fierce . . . when he had wanted to seize Anne and shake her out of that maddening aloofness. There were nights, too, when he felt an overwhelming melancholy.

Lately he had begun to think of fame, but his vision of fame was both human and fine. He had opened his eyes, looked about him; felt the hustling and contemptuous shoulders of the great city. He had seen the placards; [ 180 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

the sky signs; watched the hurrying people; heard the loud warning cries of an overcrowded universe. 

And now it was Anne who worried him. How little he knew of her, the real Anne!

Over his solitary dinner he fell into a contemplation of her hidden self. There was so much of her that was secret, elusive, unknown. She was clever . . . yes! and subtle. There were times when she made him feel like a naughty little boy, and because he was proud and shy, this power of hers infuriated him. To love did not mean to understand. Perhaps it made for misunderstanding, a blurring of the personality . . . conflict, repression?

She had looked at him with surprised questioning and told him, in that cool little voice of hers, that she did not know anything about it and that in any case, Pip was rather fond of playing to the gallery and that one must not take too much notice of her. "I don't know what you mean by that, Anne, but it sounds . . . vulgar, and unkind . . "9 He had left her then and gone downstairs to 'phone Lillian to inquire if Pip had arrived home safely. It appeared that neither Lillian nor Adolphe had returned, so Pip had reached home before them and the maid who answered him hinted that she would keep the incident to herself. Roger hung up the receiver with a sigh of relief. [181]

There was this bungalow down the river that Anne seemed rather keen on ... he supposed they would go down there, although he wasn't fond of the river ... too dull, too fashionable at week-ends. He preferred the sea and an unbroken line of sandy beach and sometimes a small boat with a thin, white sail. . . .

Bathing, too, in the deep, green water.

It seemed an eternity since she had said good-bye to Rollo. It amazed her to discover how much the thought of him disturbed her; caused her restlessness and a thousand vague longings. The night became darker, more sultry, and threatened rain. Roger pressed his foot harder upon the accelerator and watched the hand of the speedometer touch sixty. He knew that he must see Anne to-night. He was tired of this eternal hide and seek and this foolish game of cross purposes. . . They were drifting rapidly through indifference and vague misunderstandings. Up to now he'd been a fool, had let things slide because important business claimed his full attention; but now, Anne's indifference had galvanized him into slow anger and impatience and there was no more time to be lost in putting matters right.

It was after eleven o'clock when the car drew into

He looked at the still water and at the sky of inky blackness that threatened rain, and at the sadness of the willows and the fields. It was the very setting for his mood, somber and inevitable, deep, with a calm unswerving intensity. There was no break in his purpose, just as there was no break in the sky, yet he was conscious of a curious reluctance, of a slow desire moving in a world of foreboding.

CHAPTER I 

ARKES surveyed the dinner table with pride and approbation. He had attended to the details himself and was looking about him for the last time to make sure that no item had been forgotten. 

He bent forward and adjusted an embroidered tablemat and as he did so his appreciative eyes caught the gleam of silver and crystal against the polished surface of the table.

There were only three places laid and Parkes eyed the top end of the table with swift anxiety. He felt sure in his mind that young Mrs. Fordyce would dine with her husband to-night to celebrate his wonderful success in the Carter case.

Parkes was a perfect butler, but beneath his gravely deferential manner there lay an affectionate interest in the affairs of his young master and mistress. Lately he had been vaguely puzzled by the continued absence of Mrs. Fordyce and had found it increasingly difficult to suppress surprised comment amongst the other servants of the house. It seemed to him that during such an important crisis in her husband's career her presence in town was inevitable.

To-day the Carter trial had been brought to a close by the acquittal of the accused woman. The name of 

Roger Fordyce was in every paper and upon every lip, for his brilliant speech in the defence of Mrs. Carter had [207]

WHITE PIERROT 

amazed even the more hardened advocates of the law. 

To one and all his future success was definitely assured. 

Roger's father had journeyed to London and stayed at Harrington Gardens, visiting the court every day with his son. Parkes had wondered again why Mrs. Fordyce did not return from Marlowe to act as hostess and imagined that young Mr. Fordyce must have found considerable difficulty in explaining her absence. 

And now Parkes felt sure that something was wrong, for only that morning his master had ordered dinner for three, naming the third guest as Sir David Ross. "And Madam will dine here, too?" he had asked eagerly, but the answer had been sharply in the negative. 

All day long telegrams and messages had poured in, and the house in Harrington Gardens, usually so quiet, had seethed with activity.

As Parkes put the final touches to the dinner table, the door bell rang loudly. He stepped unhurriedly into the lounge and crossed to the main door. A short man with grey hair and a florid complexion brushed past him and subsided into the nearest vacant chair. "I've had a terrible day, Parkes very tiring bring me a whiskey and soda will you?" "Mr. Roger will be following, I presume, sir?" queried

Parkes, as he bent over the decanter and syphon on a side table and Henry Fordyce nodded slowly. "Yes, he will be here at any moment, so will Sir David." "It was wonderful news, sir . . . . . the verdict." "Yes, Parkes, and you should have heard my boy 

Well, I never heard anything like it. . 

Fordyce smiled and mopped his brow. "I'm very glad, sir » Henry we are all very proud of Mr. [208]

WHITE PIERROT 

Roger." And Parkes, having in his own estimation overstepped himself in the matter of conversation, turned once more to the dining-room. 

Henry Fordyce went up to his room to change and returned to the lounge in time to greet Sir David Ross. 

Whilst sipping cocktails, they fell to discussing the Carter case which was undoubtedly uppermost in the minds of everyone on this particular day.

To those who had sat in court the overwhelming eloquence of Roger Fordyce had proved the most sensational feature of the whole case. Entirely devoid of affectation or mannerisms he had overthrown the mountain of evidence piled up against the prisoner and told them a long, but simple story. It was the story of a woman's life; of her faith and devotion to a worthless husband; of her vain and pathetic efforts to keep her home together; of her futile struggles against the insane brutality of this man.

Palely eager, he had stood there in that crowded criminal court and told his story, dramatizing the thing by the stripped quietness of his telling; forcing home to the men and women of the jury the impossibility of the prisoner having ever contemplated or committed murder. 

Finally he had emphasized the fact that the prosecution had been unable to prove their case against Mrs. Carter; that they had attempted to hang a woman upon pure circumstantial evidence.

After the close of the speech for the defence, the prosecuting counsel had risen and paid tribute to the eloquence of their "learned friend," but begged the jury to judge the case on the facts placed before them, not on the moral aspect of the case. They had to judge whether or not [209]

WHITE PIERROT 

this woman had poisoned her husband and all extraneous circumstances connected with the case were irrelevant. 

After the summing up by the eminent judge, the jury had at last retired. They were absent nearly two hours and the excitement in court had reached a feverish pitch so that even the stentorian voice of the clerk of the court begging for silence was lost in the tumult of noise. 

But a hush fell upon the court as the jury returned and took their places and, in answer to the formal inquiry of the clerk, announced their verdict of "Not guilty."

A storm of cheering and applause broke forth from the public gallery, but the strain and suspense of the day had proved too much for the frail prisoner and she collapsed into the arms of the wardress behind her. 

Meanwhile Roger Fordyce had been mobbed in the well of the court by enthusiastic colleagues, and old Henry Fordyce had stood on the fringe of the crowd, attempting to reach his son but being quite unable to pass through the cordon of gowned and bewigged members of the law.

Finally he had made his way out of court into the July sunshine to his waiting car. Driving back to Harrington

Gardens, he had been aware of a feeling of great fatigue, for the courtroom had been hot and stuffy and the excitement had strained his nerves. Nevertheless, he had been overjoyed at the success of his son and had experienced immense satisfaction at the result of the case; for he knew quite well that such a brief would have proved difficult for an established barrister to handle, therefore double credit was due to a young man who had been unfamiliar with the criminal side of the law. 

Sir David Ross and Henry Fordyce had been talking [210]

WHITE PIERROT for some time before Roger finally appeared. He came into the lounge looking pale and tired and heavy about the eyes, as if he had not slept for many nights. He greeted the two men with a faint smile and turned to go upstairs. "Don't bother to change, old man. you must be very tired," said Sir David gently, but Roger had already begun to climb the stairs. "I'd rather, if you won't mind waiting a few minutes. . . . It'll freshen me up a bit."

Up in his room Roger peeled off his clothes and turned on the water in the adjoining bathroom. He still occupied the room that he and Anne had always shared, and it seemed that its feminine appointments made Anne's presence seem almost real.

He was conscious of an unutterable weariness of both mind and body, for the nervous strain of the last week had taken its toll of him and it had only been sheer determination that had enabled him to carry on to the end of the case. There was something in him that refused to recognize defeat; something like a goading spirit that cried incessantly: "You've lost Anne, so success doesn't matter to you now. . . . But this in the dock is on trial for her life down ... poor woman you can't let her you've got to go on and fight for her."

And so he had fought on and on and the people in the court had become mere puppets and the face of the woman in the dock had been the face of Anne. Sometimes he had seen her face so clearly that he had been afraid and had turned away and closed his eyes. 

The words of his speech had flowed from his lips at random, unrehearsed, almost unrealized, and in all that [ 211 ]

WHITE PIERROT 

crowded courtroom no one, not even his father, knew of the torture that had racked his mind, a torture almost equal to that suffered by the woman in the dock. 

But he had won his case in spite of everything and Mrs. Carter had been discharged by judge and jury. 

There had followed the adulation of an enthusiastic crowd and the congratulations of his colleagues. He had stood there and smiled, hating it all . . . hating it fiercely because all this success meant nothing to him now that he had lost Anne.

It was all over now and he was home again, and downstairs there was his father who must be told, sooner or later, about the break between himself and Anne. There were others who would have to know, too. Lillian and Pip and their various friends . . . and later, perhaps, the public. He shuddered and suddenly felt a profound hatred for everything in life. . • • Looking back it amazed him to realize that there had been another man in Anne's life back there in

Monte Carlo whilst she had still been engaged to him. 

The thought of it hurt him unbearably, for although he had always been aware of Anne as a typically modern girl with all the modern girl's frank sophistication, he had thought her incapable of intrigue. He could hardly understand why she had not told him about it prior to their marriage-it would have made things so much easier if they could have faced the situation squarely. The knowledge that she loved another man would still have hurt him, just as it did now, but at least they could have broken their engagement and given Anne her chance of happiness. But now? Well-marriage ties were more difficult to break than an engagement; one could not [212]

WHITE PIERROT dash into divorce proceedings without thinking things out clearly.

There was Pip, too—she came into the picture somehow, for Anne had said something about her confession being for "his sake and Pip's . . ." He didn't understand that, quite. There seemed to be a grave misunderstanding somewhere. Now that the Carter case had ended, he would have to sit down and think matters out, calmly and clearly. It was characteristic of Roger that, in relation to Anne's confession, he had never once thought of himself—yet it had meant so much to him, hearing those simply spoken words; had crushed his hope and faith and made his love for her seem a mockery and his efforts to succeed so futile. He had tried vainly to struggle against the black disillusion that overwhelmed him, but the pain and disappointment lingered in his heart and made life seem a hopeless experiment. 

As he flung himself into his dinner jacket, Roger felt again the subtle proximity of Anne and cursed himself for continuing to occupy this room. He observed for the hundredth time the array of glass-topped bottles on the dressing table and caught a glimpse of fur-edged mules that peeped from beneath the bed. There were photographs of her smiling at him from the bureau, and a thin mauve wrap lay carelessly across a chair. 

He went down to dinner feeling tired and worried and racked by a pain in his head that had lasted for some days.

CHAPTER II 

 NNE steered the punt close to the island and moored it at the foot of the shallow wooden steps leading up to the garden of "Journey's End." A thin stream of water dripped from the punt pole and splashed across the blue and orange cushions as she bent forward and pushed it through the leather straps on the outside of the boat. 

Overhead the sky was blue-grey and elusive, tricking the eyes with a promise of sunshine, whilst a light breeze ruffled the smooth calm of the water. Somewhere downstream a small sailing boat tick-tacked from side to side, its crimson sail clear cut against the greenish-grey of the river.

As Anne climbed the steps to the lawn above, Elise, her maid, met her with a telegram and Anne dropped the things she was carrying and tore open the orange flap. 

She read the following message: 

"WILL BE DOWN TO SEE YOU TO-MORROW, FRIDAY. 

"ROGER." 

She breathed a sigh of relief as she thrust the telegram into the pocket of her blue frock. It was four weeks since Roger and his father had left for Devonshire and [219]

WHITE PIERROT 

during that time the former had not once written to her or given her any intimation of his future plans. Anne scarcely remembered a more miserable four weeks in all her life, but she had stayed on at the bungalow rather than face the inevitable queries of people in town. 

Lillian had written occasionally and the Randalls had motored down and stayed a week-end with her, but for the most part she had been quite alone, happy in the knowledge that each day brought Rollo nearer to her. 

Through the long, warm days she sat and dreamed of him, seeing him everywhere; realizing that her memories of him saved her from an unbearable loneliness and despair.

There were times when she thought of Roger and was vaguely afraid, yet with her fear there was mingled a slow, warm sympathy, for despite that vivid recollection of him and Pip leaning together on that low divan beneath the window of Lillian's flat, she was not wholly convinced of his love for Pip; it seemed to her now that he had drifted into boyish infatuation with the latter, because her pathetic helplessness had appealed to his chivalrous nature. She imagined him to be quite unaware of the gradual lapse and probably intensely puzzled by it all, so that her dramatic confession on that memorable night five weeks ago had proved just as much of a shock to him as if Pip had never existed.

Anne still realized the inevitability of a divorce, and being, even in this crisis, essentially analytical, she had begun to weigh the details at the back of her mind. She had decided that as soon as Rollo returned to London she would go away with him and thus leave Roger free to file his petition; in this way there would be no stigma [220]

WHITE PIERROT 

attached to his newly gained reputation, and Anne would leave England forever. She didn't care, somehow, if she never saw London again; it was dull, uninteresting, inhabited by stodgy, unimaginative people. There were other lands across the sea, warm, sunny, colorful, beckoning adventure. It seemed to her sometimes that Rollo held the key of a prison door against which she clamored frantically for release, whilst through the grille she glimpsed part of a world she had never seen before. 

It was an ordinary, human world, yet different in a thousand ways to the one in which she lived. There was vivid sunshine and white houses and a very blue sea rippling against miles of golden sand and gardens everywhere full of bright wild flowers. The people in this enchanted world were the people Anne dreamed of meeting-gay, laughing, passionate people who held on to life with both hands. But where was this other world? 

Anne wasn't quite sure. It might have been Monte Carlo, or the isle of Capri, or parts of Italy that Rollo had described to her, but wherever it was, there was color and beauty and the promise of adventure and desperately 

Anne still longed for them. Until her meeting with

Rollo she had gone alone in quest of adventure, but she thought now of her future life with him; a new freedom; the fulfillment of her wildest dreams . . . and love, love such as she had never thought existed. 

As she sat on the little verandah overlooking the river, eating her lunch, she thought determinedly: "I was afraid of loving Rollo. afraid of flouting convention and but now, I know I've learned my lesson security • · and I realize that nothing could be more ghastly than [221]

WHITE PIERROT this smug respectability, this pretence and loneliness. I think I'd rather die than let it go on forever."

During the afternoon the postman brought her a letter bearing the Devonshire postmark and, opening it, she recognized the neat handwriting of Henry Fordyce. "MY DEAR ANNE,- "I hope that you will receive this letter before Roger reaches Marlowe, as I am most anxious that you and he should forget the foolish quarrel between you and resume your life together. "You have no idea, Anne, how much Roger has been worried by the probability of divorce between you and there is no doubt that his health is suffering greatly. "I am not aware of the full details of the case, but I do urge you to drop the idea of separation and divorce, as I feel sure that you would both regret it later. I am not taking sides with either of you, as you are both of an age when you must fight your own battles; but I do suggest that the unhappiness you have caused Roger is a very poor reward for the hard work he has done. 

There is a big future ahead for him, but you know as well as I do that he cannot give proper attention to his career whilst he is laboring under this intense nervous strain. "You need have no fear regarding Roger's affection for you. He is as sincere in that as he is in all things. "I'd like to have a chat with you, Anne, as I [222]

WHITE PIERROT 

might be able to help you; meanwhile be kind to Roger and if you can, go back to town with him to-morrow. "Don't let him know I've written you; he'd hate my interference, but I couldn't stand by and see you make fools of yourselves. Remember, if there is anything I can do, you have only to let me know. "Yours affectionately, 

"HENRY FORDYCE." 

Anne smiled a little as she slipped the letter between the pages of her book. It was rather an absurd letter, she thought, in that it showed a complete ignorance of the true state of affairs between herself and Roger and she decided that, as soon as she had seen Roger, she would write to his father and impress upon him the impossibility of her marriage to Roger ever proving the success that she had hoped.

She stared across the water and her eye caught the downward swoop of a waterfowl as it flew amongst the reeds on the opposite bank of the river. The next moment she heard a voice calling her from the garden of the next bungalow and turned to face the anxious eyes of 

Mrs. Jordan's maid. "Is anything the matter?" she asked quickly. "I think my mistress is ill," replied the girl in a whisper. "I'll come round and see if I can help.”

Anne had become rather friendly with the occupant of the adjoining bungalow during her stay at Marlowe, and had often marvelled at the bliss and contentment that [223]

WHITE PIERROT 

filled Mrs. Jordan's life. Mr. Jordan was a tall, thin young man who travelled to the City every day and who, incidentally, did not appear to the critical eyes of Anne to be blessed with the various qualities of character with which his pretty little wife endowed him, but he apparently made her happy and therefore served his purpose in life.

She found Mrs. Jordan reclining on a settee in the sitting-room, looking pale and drawn, one limp hand clasping a bottle of smelling salts.

Anne shook her head. "No, Mrs. Jordan will be better in a few moments. I think the heat must have upset her."

"In ten years' time she will look just the same . . . it is

It was early evening on the following day.

Through the dim light in the little sitting-room Roger and Anne faced each other. The remnants of the evening meal on the dinner table lay between them and both smoked cigarettes in silence. The French windows were wide open and admitted glimpses of passing craft on the [226]

WHITE PIERROT 

river and in the garden there were shadows and an evening mist over the flowers.

For over an hour they had sat there, these two, and talked of trivialities. It was Anne who had purposely ignored the one subject that was uppermost in the minds of them both and by doing so had cleverly saved the situation from absurd drama. It was obvious that she was the calmer of the two, despite the fact that her nerves had undergone considerable strain during the past twenty-four hours. Nearly all night she had lain half awake and through the darkness had conjured up visions of herself and Rollo running all over the world, haunted and pursued and desperately unhappy. She had sat up in the gloom and whimpered like a frightened child and for the hundredth time envied Eileen Jordan. Then she remembered the trim little bungalow owned by the Jordans; the ascetic looking husband, and the prospective baby. She had shuddered then because they represented the smug security from which she longed to escape. 

When the morning had come, she had looked up at the rain-washed sky and seen the smiling face of Rollo. He had smiled a little contemptuously, she thought, perhaps because he was amused at her childish fears.

It was just an overwhelming, torrential impulse that was both physical and spiritual. He wanted to see her, to be sure that she still lived and was near him. 

Sometimes he despised himself for letting her go so easily, "without a fight" as he had said, but how could he fight, when, in any case, Anne no longer wanted him?

He could not hold her against her will any more than he could seek out this lover of hers and kill him! In real life one didn't adopt such drastic measures-if you were an ordinary, everyday man, you had to accept defeat, swallow your pride, and retire as gracefully as possible.

It was queer to realize that he was alone now, and free to do as he pleased. He remembered that in the old days it had been a struggle to give up his game of golf and the quiet jaunts in the car and allow himself to be dragged off to foolish parties and night clubs. But now, the prospect of returning to his old mode of living filled him with desperation. He would have liked to have gone abroad somewhere for a long holiday, but important law work detained him in town for some considerable time. For people like him there was no escape. One worked a little harder, perhaps, in order to keep quite sane. He tried not to think of Anne, because the memory of her still caused him unbearable pain. He could scarcely realize that he would never see her again; would never kiss those sweet lips and stroke that small gold [237]

"The house here is at your disposal from now onwards. Unfortunately I am obliged to run down to Devonshire again, as my father has been taken ill suddenly. "On my return I shall stay at the Club, as I expect that Journey's End will have been taken over by its owners. "With best wishes. "Yours sincerely, "ROGER." 

He sealed the letter and gave it to Parkes to address and ten minutes later was on his way to the garage.

She powdered her nose and drank the weak tea that Elise brought her, and her thoughts dwelt on Rollo again and the wonderful realization that she was going to see him again after seven long weeks. The journey down to Bury was long and tedious and it was late afternoon when she finally arrived at Weston Hall. A car had been sent to meet her and as it turned into the long drive that led up to the house, she caught sight of beds of flowers, a swimming pool, and an elaborate Dutch garden. The house itself, an old country mansion, was very picturesque with its faded red brick walls, its gabled roofs and high lattice windows. 

She was ushered into a wide, oak-beamed lounge, where Lady Weston rose to greet her, the remainder of the guests having already retired to their rooms to dress for dinner.

They talked for a few minutes and Lady Weston then excused herself and went upstairs. Anne followed a maid up to a large room on the first floor. She was thinking anxiously as she looked about her: "Lady Weston did not mention Rollo. Suppose he isn't here? 

Suppose he doesn't come after all?"

The room overlooked the tennis courts at the back of the house and a copse of fir trees beyond. It was furnished in pale weathered oak, with chairs and covers of flowered tapestry and an old fashioned four-poster bed. 

There were rough walls, yellow distempered, and overhead the ceiling was composed of huge beams of dark, unvarnished oak. The windows were long and high, and a wide seat had been built beneath them so that one could rest there and look down upon a view of unusual beauty. -I- P IP looked down at the smooth tongue of water that crept up between the rocks and lapped in tiny wavelets over the pebbles at her feet. Deepening twilight cast purple shadows over the sea and down the coastline towards Salcombe Bay giant boulders of rock were blackly silhouetted against the fading sunset of orange and gold. 

The sea stretched out to a dim horizon and Pip watched the gaunt old fishing boats moving slowly shoreward, their brownish red sails clear cut against the skyline. 

She loved the peace and quietness and beauty of it all. 

Something of the great calm of the sea had entered her heart and the majesty of the rocks filled her with a childish awe. She sat there clasping her hands and staring seaward. "To-morrow is my last day.. of it." ... I can't bear to think

"This is probably the last letter I shall write you, as I understand that the solicitors are shortly taking up our case and from that time onward all correspondence between us must cease. I take this opportunity, therefore, of wishing you every happiness for the future. "I might add that I can still hardly believe that the Anne I knew and loved could act as you have done, but, of course, I have no right to discuss your actions. "Yours, "ROGER." 

Over and over again she read that last part until the very words burned like fire in her brain. She wondered why Roger had written these lines to a letter that was [265]

WHITE PIERROT 

otherwise quite impersonal. "He wants me to know that he is still hurt," she said to herself, and added dully: "And ever since I came down here I've been worried about him. Why? Oh, why? I've planned everything so that the full blame falls upon me. Yet the whole thing seems cheap and mean just as if Rollo and I were fugitives from justice. I could have saved Roger all the scandal of divorce if I had only told him everything before we were married. But I told myself that it would be hurting him too much . . . and letting him down. Instead of that, it was because I was afraid . 

I was a coward . . . I always have been a coward and now I'm paying for it. I wanted marriage and security. 

I had no faith in Rollo or his love. 

Anne watched the sunlight glimmer through the trees and fall in long diagonal beams across the sunken garden. 

And over her shoulder a voice mocked her: "It's new to see yourself like this, isn't it? Very ordinary and dreadful. Perhaps it will teach you not to be mean about other people 99 She hung her head and a queer bitterness entered her heart. It seemed as if she had suddenly come face to face with herself and hated the ordeal. And in this fleeting moment of recognition Anne spared herself nothing, for she was a girl who took life as it was and held it passionately while looking toward the ultimate shadows. 

She saw the whole of her life as a series of vain searchings for adventure, just pathetic rushings to and fro after the passing of the semi-savagery and vague rebelliousness that is childhood. Both Roger and Rollo had been the victims of her passionate quest and to both she felt she owed something as reparation. [266]

WHITE PIERROT 

Had it not been for her unexpected meeting with Rollo in London, she felt that her marriage would have remained a permanent institution and the uneventful years would have passed on until old age and maturity stilled her further cravings for adventure. She had not dreamed that she would ever meet Rollo again, yet he had said, in his boyish, passionate voice: “One day I shall come back to you. Your marriage can make no difference."

He had stood on a white balcony high above the sea and behind him the moon had smiled and a yellow room had turned to gold. At the foot of the headland, a car had waited for her whilst she said good-bye and in it had sat an Englishman, tall and very cool, who had brought her back from an enchanted world into the cold, bare realities of life. The realities had been Roger and Harrington Gardens; a dull honeymoon; married life and domestic routine.

And now it was all over and she would soon be free again. At least, not really free because there was Rollo to whom she had promised the rest of her life. Her future lay in his hands. 

And suddenly Anne wished that she could die. She hated life because she couldn't understand it-it was too complex; she hated Roger because he made her feel cowardly and ashamed; she hated Rollo because of his power over her, and lastly she hated herself because for the first time she realized her own selfishness, her weakness, her worthlessness. Yet even in this wave of bitter self-analysis Anne remained a brave and pathetic figure. 

She sat pressed hard against the old wooden seat with the sunlight streaming into her wide green eyes and running upward to the roots of her tawny-gold hair. [267]

WHITE PIERROT

CHAPTER V 

HE following day Anne had begun preparations for her departure to Paris. It had been arranged that she and Rollo were to leave London on the Thursday evening by car, picking up the night boat at Dover. They had abandoned the idea of making the journey separately and meeting in Paris, and Anne had also vetoed the train journey direct from Victoria bearing in mind the dread possibility of there being people she knew travelling on the same train. 

Anne had returned to Harrington Gardens the previous evening. Throughout the journey from Weston

There were open trunks lying about the floor; piles of clothes on the bed and an alarming array of hats and shoes on adjacent stools and chairs.

Policemen eyed her suspiciously as she hesitated in the rain outside the entrances and one of them said rudely: "Nothing much on to-day, miss. Court of criminal appeal sits on Mondays."

She passed through the main entrance and found the stone stairs leading to the courtrooms above. There were very few people about and her swift footsteps echoed dully along the dimly lighted corridors. Occasionally, a dark figure hurried past her, gowned in flowing black, with curled wig gleaming whitely in the darkness.

She paused outside each courtroom and read the cases listed for the day, together with the names of counsel briefed. A kindly official finally directed her to the court where Roger sat in the midst of an important civil case. 

She crept noiselessly into a seat at the back of the room and looked about her anxiously, but her entrance had not attracted undue attention, for an important witness was in the throes of cross-examination and the interest of everyone in court was diverted to the witness-box. Opposite her, on a long, high dais sat the scarlet robed judges, heads bent over piles of papers and books of reference, as they listened intently to the proceedings.

Anne thought it all rather dull, yet in spite of this the atmosphere thrilled her just as it had done on another occasion, long ago, when she had sat and listened to Roger's first case. She remembered that it had been a case similar to this and equally dull, but her whole atten- [277]

WHITE PIERROT 

tion had been centered upon Roger. He had seemed so different then; so much younger; so much keener and so very conscious of the responsibility upon his shoulders. 

Looking at him now, as he rose to his feet and began to address the judges, she scarcely recognized him as the same man; he had poise and self-assurance and a manner that was wholly convincing. Even his voice had changed; had become deeper, somehow, and more compelling. 

She listened to him with renewed interest, straining forward to catch every word, yet she understood only a few odd phrases here and there, for he delved deeply into the complicated details of Company Law. She found herself admiring certain things about him, just as if he were a person whom she saw for the first time, while at the back of her mind a thought came swiftly: "He is clever. One day they will make him a K.C."

It seemed ironical to her that she should sit there, unknown to him, and look upon him for the last time. 

The last time. She shivered a little. It sounded ominous, somehow, and horribly final. Ever since her return from 

Weston Hall she had been overwhelmed by an irresistible desire to see him again and to hear his voice which even his refusal to meet her (so curt, she had thought) had failed to subdue. And now that she had gratified her wish, she was conscious of a sense of disappointment because it seemed to her that all her sympathy for him had been misplaced and that her visions of his distress had merely been the foolish illusions of an uneasy mind. 

There came a lull in the proceedings when a loud voice boomed: "The court will adjourn until two o'clock, please!"

He walked slowly, with head erect and the lines of his face grimly set. He passed within two feet of where she sat and she felt against her clenched hands the cool draught made by the sweeping of his voluminous gown. And then he had gone.

The room was empty now. The lights had been turned off and fitful daylight crept in between the drawn curtains and cast eerie shadows over the polished desks and panelled walls.

Anne rose to her feet and passed hurriedly into the deserted corridor outside. Her face was rather white and she stared ahead with a queer expression in her eyes, for her thoughts were still with Roger and she was conscious of a bitterness and despair within her that she could not understand.

She made her way out into the daylight and walked toward the Strand. The pavements were crowded with people; the swarms of City workers hurrying to their mid-day meal. Anne found herself in the midst of a vast human tide; heard laughter and voices about her and the steady march of feet. The traffic surged close against the pavements, splashing mud on to the legs and clothes of unwary pedestrians. A chill wind blew up from the river, but Anne, struggling westward, felt unpleasantly [ 279 ]

She remembered another day, long ago, when a carnivalmad crowd had nearly crushed her to death; the close proximity of warm, damp bodies had sickened her then just as it did now. She shuddered and climbed into a taxi that had slowed up against the curb. 

In Regent Street she alighted and walked towards Maddox Street, intending to stop and buy some shoes. 

She supposed, idly, that it would be rather difficult to buy decent shoes in Italy and one couldn't leave everything until one arrived in Paris. Besides, she hated asking for size fives in Paris; they looked at you as if you ought to be ashamed of having such enormous feet!

Her thoughts flew back again to Roger. Somehow she had not expected to find him so exclusively engrossed in his work, almost as if nothing had happened. Thousands of women have suffered in the same way and thousands will go on after you. It is all very ordinary and very typical of men like Roger; for to them 'love is a thing apart"."

Pip knew that it was just the kind of advice that she wanted to hear, but in her heart there were other forces at war which would not allow her to accept such an answer to the problem.

And time was short. It wasn't as if there were even a few days in which to make up one's mind-it was simply a matter of hours now before Anne was due to leave town. Once she had left, there was no recalling her, Pip knew. There was also a great probability that in any case she would not alter her plans. Anne had pride and determination and was the kind of person who would see anything through to the very end once the die was cast.

Oh, vain, futile quest! And now... .. death! Death! 

Pip, like a thin white candle-flame, suddenly snuffed out! 

Oh, God!

And long afterwards there was a movement beside her and she felt a hand on her shoulder. Without lifting her head she said: "Go away, Rollo. please go away." "My dear, I want you to try and explain ... 

Explain! Explain! And suddenly she was helpless with laughter. Wild insane laughter that rang through the little room and sounded like blasphemy in a quiet church. The last sounds of it died away in a gasp of anguish, and, looking up, she met the dark eyes above her through a veil of tears. 

And something Rollo saw in the white face before him checked the words that rose to his lips. Perhaps, in that moment, they both realized that any sort of good-bye between them must sound absurdly melodramatic. Anne felt the swift pressure of warm fingers upon her own. It was a final gesture.

Far beyond her, in the shapeless realities by the door, there was a careful sound. When, at last, she lifted her head again, she was alone. [310]

CHAPTER VII 

PON a certain morning one month later, Roger Fordyce called to Parkes as he was about to leave for the City. "I've just had a letter from Mrs. Fordyce, Parkes, to say that she will be home this evening. I should like you to arrange a special dinner for us and put plenty of flowers about the place." er er • • 

The eyes of Parkes gleamed behind their horn-rimmed spectacles. "Very good, sir. Is there anything else, sir?”

Roger glanced back over his shoulder and smiled. It was weeks since he had smiled and at the sight of his master's face Parkes felt an inward happiness. "No, Parkes, I can't think of anything else." "Very good, sir. I hope Madam has quite recovered, sir ?"

The smile broadened; deepened into a boyish grin. "She has quite recovered, thank you, Parkes. We had suggested, her aunt and I, that she should take a long sea voyage, but Mrs. Fordyce apparently prefers to return home." "Oh, I'm very glad, sir, to be sure." "So am I. Well, good morning, Parkes.” "Good morning, sir."

Driving to the city Roger remembered certain lines in Anne's letter that had puzzled him. He found himself [311]

WHITE PIERROT wondering at the real reason for her peremptory return to Harrington Gardens. She had written: "I shall be home on Friday, Roger, and if you are willing to forgive everything and start again, I will promise to do my very best in the future. After all, we owe this to Pip. You must realize that . . ."

It all seemed to him a little ambiguous. He could not be sure whether Anne meant to return to him because she loved him or whether it was because of a sense of duty toward Pip.

As regards forgiving Anne, he had no hesitation about that. As Pip had once said to him: "If you really love 

Anne, you will forgive anything." 

Yes, his love was big enough for that. Sometimes he had felt that his love had been submerged in an overwhelming jealousy because of that "other man." But that was all over now. What a fool he had been to make himself wretched over a man like Rollo Savini. He found it absurdly easy to overlook Savini; to ignore Savini; to forget Savini.

Perhaps he had fully realized the extent of his love for Anne only when he had seen her so completely broken by the tragic death of Pip. Something in her pathetic helplessness had moved him strangely and his passionate need of her had returned in the guise of an intolerable tenderness.

And now he was looking forward to her return with an almost schoolboyish eagerness. Meanwhile, there was important work to be done in the City. An ugly case of blackmail scheduled for hearing to-day required his undi- [312]

WHITE PIERROT 

vided attention, but he was happy, happier than he had been for months and years, and Danvers heard him singing an old war ditty as he came leaping up the stairs to the office.

It was late October and the days were becoming shorter and darker. Even Harrington Gardens and the quiet square had lost their pristine freshness and looked a little bleak with brown leaves scurrying along in the wind and the branches of the trees showing grim and dark against the sky.

It was just before six o'clock that evening that Anne alighted from a taxi with her maid, Elise, and rang the bell at No. 9. Her welcome from Parkes, as the door was flung open, would have done justice to a lady of royal blood and Anne could scarcely refrain from smiling. 

The very house seemed to greet her warmly and as she moved toward the huge log fire that leapt upon the open hearth in the lounge, the thought came to her swiftly: "How I love this place. It always welcomes me . . . just like a real person . . going to sit by the fire, Elise, so you can take my things up. I shan't bother to change to-night. 

She turned to her maid: "I'm 

She slipped a long fur coat from her shoulders and peeled a tiny felt hat from her head. There was a bright spot of color upon each cheek and her eyes were very clear and very green. She wore a slim-fitting frock, almost the color of her eyes and against it her curly hair gleamed like burnished gold. Very fit, she looked, and surprisingly good-looking.

Parkes brought her a cocktail and informed her that the master would be home at any minute. Sipping the [313]

W HITE PIERROT 

mixed vermouth as she sat on a wide settee near the fire, she tried to remember something that Hugh Randall had once said about cocktails. "It is the clinking of the glasses that makes for sociability, not the contents thereof." She remembered that everybody had laughed at that and somebody had pointed out that Hugh preferred Clover Club! The thought was irrelevant, but at this moment it served to amuse her. 

She glanced about the darkened lounge and saw dancing lights and shadows on polished floor and panelled walls. She liked it like this. Just firelight. She found the tears swimming into her eyes again; found herself weeping again, as she had wept so often lately, silently, weakly, uncontrollably. She hardly knew why she cried. 

There was love in her tears, that was all she knew; an immense love that stretched far beyond the frontiers of a personal love; a love that was like a dark and beautiful river sweeping over the jagged rocks of life and death and embracing in its depths the futility of the world. 

Yet it seemed, somehow, a beautiful futility. 

There was a sound by the door and she heard a latchkey being turned in the lock. She wondered vaguely why 

Roger did not ring the bell. And then, looking upward, she saw him standing in the doorway. Glad that he could not see her face, she stared at him. She understood now. That other love had gone from her; that mad, dream love; that selfish love that is born of loneliness.

And as she rose to greet him, she knew that they would be together always; that their lives were bound henceforth by a law over which they had no control. 

He came forward into the circle of firelight and looked at her. He saw the tears still wet upon her lashes and [314]

WHITE PIERROT 

the soft line about her mouth that was almost a smile. 

And somehow, he found himself bereft of speech. The whole place seemed to shine with her. He could think of nothing but of being just here with her. 

Thus they stared at each other in a helplessness that could find no point of contact. 66 .. Roger ." said Anne, at last.

There was something in her little cry that stirred him to life and he moved forward. She clung to him and shut her eyes. Her whole heart and spirit were acquiescent . . . at peace. She would have no regrets now. 

All of life was before them. Life- 

He could have none. the glorious contest-no longer the bitter endeavor.
